0br. **The full cycle account, from the ISA rather than from the counters -- and it reorders the whole backlog: stalls are 36% of the time and worth four times more than every instruction we could remove.** (user 2026-09-30 "go", after 0bq killed the counter-based model.)

**The instrument.** `tools/isa_blocks.py` reads a `DEEPMOE_PIPELINE_STATS` dump and reports the
static instruction mix per basic block, weighted by trip counts the caller supplies, split into
WMMA / VOPD / other VALU / SALU / global load / LDS read / LDS write / waitcnt / barrier. It
exists because the hardware instruction counters under-report (0bq) while the ISA does not: the
dump is exact, and the trip counts are known from the shape. A `v_dual_*` encoding is reported
as one issue slot carrying two operations, and both figures are printed, because which one
matters depends on whether you are counting issue slots or work.

**The loop body of the real kernel** (fp16 variant of the gate/up shape 2304x5120, wm4 wn2,
`LDS_BK` 64, patched driver, 80 K slices):

| block | what it does | per slice | of which |
|---|---|---|---|
| BB1 | write the prefetched slice into LDS | 38 issue | 24 VOPD, 12 `ds_write_b128`, **2 `s_barrier`**, 13 `s_waitcnt` |
| BB2 | form the addresses for the next 12 global loads | 73 issue | 60 VALU, 12 `global_load` |
| BB4 | read LDS, pack fragments, multiply | 117 issue | **32 `v_wmma`**, 33 VOPD, 48 `ds_read_b128`, 20 `s_waitcnt` |
| BB5 | loop-carried moves | 32 issue | 32 VOPD |
| BB6 | back edge | 7 issue | 7 VOPD |
| | **total** | **267 issue** | **32 WMMA = 12%** |

Per wave that is 21,804 issue slots, 2,560 of them WMMA, plus 2,883 `s_waitcnt` and 160
`s_barrier`.

**The account.** A WMMA coordinates 32 clocks of work on its SIMD32 and gfx1151 has 80 of them
(0bq). Charging every other encoding one clock, for the n=512 dispatch (576 waves, 1,131,129
measured active cycles):

| | cycles a SIMD | share |
|---|---:|---:|
| WMMA issue | 589,824 | **52.1%** |
| every other instruction's issue | 135,360 | **12.0%** |
| **neither -- stalls** | **405,945** | **35.9%** |

**What that implies, and it is the useful part.** Removing *every* non-WMMA instruction in the
kernel would take it from 52.1% to **59.2%** of peak. Removing the stalls would take it to
**81.3%**. So the entire "cut instructions" family -- codex's ranked items 2, 3 and 4, and my
own reading of 0bn -- competes for at most 7 points, while the stalls hold 29. **The
instruction count is not where the money is.** This also retires the question 0bq left open:
calibrating the hardware instruction counter is no longer on the critical path, because the ISA
answers the same question exactly and the answer is "not there".

**Where the stalls are, from the same dump**: 2 `s_barrier` and 13 `s_waitcnt` in BB1, 20
`s_waitcnt` in BB4, every slice. The barriers are the LDS staging handshake and the waitcnts are
the global loads landing. With the LDS at 27,648 B a workgroup only 2 workgroups fit a 64 KB CU
= 8 waves a CU = **4 waves a SIMD**, and the VGPR count of 256 would have allowed 6 -- so
occupancy is LDS-bound, as codex said.

**A correction to 0bn, 0bp and row 86 while I am here**: I wrote that occupancy as "2 waves a
SIMD" and BK=32's as "2 to 4". A CU has **two** SIMD32s, so a 128-thread workgroup is 4 wave32s
and the real figures are **4 waves a SIMD at BK=64 and 8 at BK=32**. Off by a factor of two
throughout; the three places are fixed in place. The qualitative story is unchanged -- doubling
occupancy made the kernel slower -- but the absolute numbers were wrong and anyone reasoning
from them would have been misled.

**The one experiment this points at.** Row 86 proved that buying occupancy by narrowing the K
slice loses, because a 32-half row read is 64 B, under the 128 B cache line, and VRAM reads
double. But those two things need not be coupled: **keep reading 128 B a row into registers as
now, and stage it into LDS in two halves of 32 halfs, running two sub-slices of WMMA per global
read.** LDS then falls to 15,360 B a workgroup -- 4 workgroups a CU, **8 waves a SIMD** -- with
the global read granularity, and therefore the VRAM traffic, exactly unchanged. It costs twice
the barriers, but with twice the waves to hide them. This is the one cell of the design space
that row 86 did not test and that the cycle account says is where the 29 points live.
Predicted, already halved: **unpriced** -- the account says the headroom is 29 points but says
nothing about how much of the stall this removes. Falsified if GPU active cycles do not fall at
the same VRAM read size, or if the extra barriers cost more than the waves recover. Cost:
hours, in the staging loop of `prefill_gemm_lds.slang`, and it changes no arithmetic, so the
bit-exactness gates are the check.

**Caveat on the whole account**: it rests on WMMA occupying 32 clocks of a SIMD32, which is
AMD's published figure for RDNA3 and is what makes the theoretical peak 59.4 TFLOP/s. The
repo's 52.8 TFLOP/s is a *measured probe* peak, 89% of that, so a ceiling stated against the
probe would be slightly lower. Charging one clock to every non-WMMA encoding is also generous
to that bucket -- SALU issues on a separate pipe and can overlap -- so 12.0% is an upper bound
and the 35.9% stall share is a lower bound.

`tools/isa_blocks.py`, `scratchpad/ps_fp4`.

0bq. **I proposed a model, falsified it with my own data, and along the way found a real defect in the instrument; what I got in exchange is a model that does not depend on the counters and that two independent routes confirm: this kernel's WMMA pipe is idle 47% of the time.** (user 2026-09-30 "go" + "have CodeX help you")

**One, the instrument's defect (fixed): the instruction-counting counters lose counts, and they only ever under-report.** The same work (wm4wn2, n=512, 576 waves, patched driver) run four times in a row gives `VALU Instructions` = **2,484,288 / 2,691,312 / 2,898,336 / 3,312,384** — each differing by exactly 207,024, and only the maximum divides into a whole number per wave; `LDS Instructions` likewise (998,400 / 1,152,000 / 1,152,000). The control group shows this is not machine noise: **`Waves` is 576 all four times, dispersion 0.0%** (and equals the number computed from the grid), while `GPU active cycles` is ±2%, `VRAM read size` ±10%, `L2 hit` ±4.5% — the jitter in those three is physical. **The fix**: repeat each group `DEEPMOE_PERF_REPEAT` times (default 3) and take the **maximum**, and print "what fraction the worst run lost" alongside. This, together with §7 0bn's "one counter at a time", is the pair of usage disciplines for this instrument.

**Two, my model, and why it was wrong.** I saw mma make up 59.4% of the VALU instruction stream while the measurement was "59% of peak", and proposed "distance from peak = mma's share of the VALU stream". **Both are wrong**: the numerator's 59.4% used a VALU count that had lost 25% (clean value 5,751/wave, mma 2,560 → 44.5%), and the denominator's 59% is 31.7 TFLOP/s divided by the **measured probe peak 52.8** — whereas the theoretical peak is **59.4 TFLOP/s** (80 SIMDs × 8192 FLOP / 32 clock × 2.9 GHz), and 52.8 is only 89% of it. The two numbers colliding was pure coincidence. **One basis worth recording in passing**: the "distance from mma peak" in several places in this file uses the probe value 52.8, which is 11% more forgiving than the theoretical value; a citation has to say which one it means.

**Three, the model I got in exchange (proposed by codex, verified by me along two routes, neither depending on the instruction counters).** One RDNA3 WMMA instruction orchestrates **32 clocks of work**, and gfx1151 has **80 SIMD32s** (40 CUs × 2). So:

```
cycles the matrix work should occupy = 576 waves x 2,560 WMMA x 32 clocks / 80 SIMD = 589,824
measured GPU active cycles                                                = 1,131,129
                                              -> WMMA utilisation = 52.1%
by throughput: 31.7 TFLOP/s / 59.4 (theoretical)                          = 53.4%
```

The two routes meet at 52–53%. **So the accurate statement is: this kernel leaves the matrix pipe idle 47–48% of the time**, and not what I wrote yesterday, "1.8× short and the limit is occupancy" (§3 86 has already falsified the occupancy half; see 0bp).

**Four, why "cut the non-mma instructions" is not a computable lever (at least not yet).** codex counted the final ISA of the fp16 variant under the patched driver: in the loop body, per slice, BB1 has 24 VOPD pairs (moving loaded values into LDS store operands), BB2 has 60 instructions (forming addresses for the next 12 global loads, 79 prefetch slices), BB4 has 36 non-WMMA instructions (packing the results read out of LDS into WMMA fragments, with 48 `ds_read_b128` + 32 WMMA in the same slice), BB6 has 7 pairs (loop-carried register copies) — **the loop body alone is about 10,093 non-WMMA vector instructions per wave**, plus 2,560 WMMA for about 12,653 in total. But the counter's clean maximum is **5,751, only 45%**. **Until that difference in basis is understood, "this instruction saves x% of peak" cannot be computed** — substituting the ISA counts into my formula yields more than 100% of peak, which is itself a falsification of the formula. So the first thing in the next step is to **calibrate the counters** (does it count WMMA at all, does a VOPD pair count as one instruction or two, is it sampled per SE/SA and therefore in need of scaling), not to change the shader.

**Five, another door closed: wave64 is not "half the waves doing twice the work".** One WMMA instruction produces only **one 16×16 tile**, whether wave32 or wave64; wave64 replicates A/B into four 16-lane groups, with each lane holding fewer output values. Two tiles still take two WMMA instructions. So it cannot be costed as "half the WMMA count"; crediting it with any gain would require compiling the wave64 ISA first and measuring it (§3 8 found no systematic difference back then on MoE GEMV, and that cannot be carried over as a conclusion for GEMM).

**Six, the reordered next steps** (codex's ordering, which I accept): ① **calibrate the counters** (take a WMMA-only minimal probe case, one counter at a time, and align against the ISA's WMMA instruction count — without touching the numerics path); ② only after ① is it worth discussing touching BB1's 24 moving pairs and BB4's 36 fragment-packing instructions, and it must be proven by an ISA diff that instructions really went away; ③ register lifetime reordering for the large tile, on the precondition of keeping BK=64 and the 128 B row reads and proving zero spills in the stats; ④ a targeted NIR/ACO pass, ranked after ② has proven there are removable sequences; ⑤ LDS bank layout needs bank-conflict or cycle evidence first, and wave priority and MALL have no premise under the current evidence.

Gates: this round changed only the bench's instrumentation (`bench/perf_query.h` repeating and taking the maximum + `prefill_bench`'s printing), and the numerics path not one byte; CPU gate 25/25. `scratchpad/ps_fp4`, `ps_bk32`.

0bp. **Two Codex reviews (user 2026-09-30 "have CodeX look over the many leftover optimisations, and also look at how to do custom optimisation"): they corrected me in two places and gave one hypothesis ranked first, and I measured that hypothesis —** the hypothesis was refuted, and **it also refutes 0bn's occupancy conclusion.**

**One, the two places codex corrected me (both verified as valid, corrected in place)**

① **In 0bo I overstated.** I wrote "expert io 12.04 s ≈ expert gpu 11.45 s, the disk is no longer the bottleneck at 17K" — but these two are **overlapping aggregate buckets, not two serial phases**. §3 80 has long recorded that under ring 6 **layers 0–19 are GPU-bound and layers 20–39 (replay) are read-limited**. The accurate statement can only be: savings on the GPU side reach wall time in the first 20 layers, and in the last 20 layers can still be covered by the reads; the ranking has to use the per-layer and per-op times, not the difference of two aggregate numbers. 0bo has been corrected.

② **I did not state 0bn's range of applicability clearly.** That run was **fp16 random weights, n=1024**, and moreover **the `wm2wn4` cell is not reachable on the platform at all** — in `pf_lds_geo`, `wn = n <= 32 ? 1 : 2`, so wn never exceeds 2, and 2304 rows take **wm4wn2** when n > 64. 0bn now carries both of these restrictions.

**Two, the premise check both reviews ranked first, which I did, and it passed.** Capturing the pipeline stats of the real **fused FP4** kernel (`LdsWFp4=1`) under the **patched driver**: gate/up 2304×5120 wm4wn2 gives **VGPR 256 / LDS 27,648 / 0 spills**, down 5120×2304 the same, **byte for byte identical to the fp16 variant**. So 0bn's occupancy characterisation (2 workgroups/CU = **4** waves/SIMD, VGPR at the ceiling) **does transfer to the real FP4 path** — this is no longer my guess.

**Three, and then the hypothesis was falsified.** The top lever in both reviews was "under the same tile geometry, squeeze LDS below 64 KB/3 in exchange for 3 workgroups". There is a version cheaper than the BK=48 they proposed: **BK=32** (both 5120 and 2304 divide evenly, so no K tail is needed, and the staging registers halve too). The result is in **§3 86**: **occupancy did double from 2 to 4 waves/SIMD (LDS 15,360, VGPR 192, 0 spills), and the kernel got up to 39% slower.**

**So "the limit is occupancy" is wrong**, and that sentence in 0bn is to be read in light of this. The counters said what the real limit is (the n=512 pairing): **VRAM read 102.7 → 200.8 MB, doubled, while the volume of data did not change** — at BK=32 each row reads 64 B per slice, **below the 128 B cache line**, so every line is fetched twice. **`LDS_BK=64` is a local optimum pinned from both sides**: upward it hits register pressure (§3 68, 81), downward it hits the cache line granularity; the causes of death in the two directions are different, so it is not something "one more round of tuning" can get past. This is written into the shader comments so the next person does not turn this knob again.

**The corrected judgement**: in 0bn's table the traffic falls monotonically with the tile while the time bottoms out in the middle — both sides getting worse now have names (large tiles spill registers, small slices waste cache lines), and the middle cell is this kernel family's optimum between 128 B row granularity and 256 VGPRs. **To go further, what has to change is the coupling itself of "how much each slice reads and how much each wave computes", not any existing knob** — the register lifetime reordering codex ranked 2nd and 3rd (`TileC` / `ra` / `rb`) is the only direction still standing, at a cost on the order of "days".

**Four, one hard fact from codex that saves a whole blind alley**: **256 VGPRs is RDNA 3.5's architectural limit, not an ACO policy** (under wave32 there are 1,536 VGPRs per SIMD, allocated in blocks of 24). Changing ACO's `MAX_VGPRS` cannot make a shader that wants 270 registers legal. So "VGPR pinned at 256" in 0bn is not a ceiling that can be raised; **the only way out is to reduce live values**. It also points out that the **binding constraint on occupancy is LDS, not VGPR** (2 workgroups because LDS only holds 2) — §3 86 confirmed this from the other side.

**Five, the stale statements codex found, each verified and fixed**: §6 9 ("GPU prefill off by default, `--gpu-prefill-min` defaults to 0" — in fact `session.h:223` defaults to 512 and `serve.cpp:362` lowers it to 16 on RADV), §6 12 (the per-dispatch trace "not yet verified on the GPU" — `engine.cpp:567` has been using it all along, and §3 49/53 are readings of it), and the sentence at the top of `prefill_gemm_lds.slang` saying "b128 is not available" (true only for stock RADV, not true after 0bl).

**Six, the knobs below the driver, which codex checked one by one against STATUS, with the conclusion that none moves this round**: DPM (§7 0j already holds `high` with GameMode), GTT/carve-out (already changed, and more GTT does not raise WMMA occupancy), THP/udmabuf (§7 has already priced the bounce copy at 48 GB/s and turned it off), `amdgpu.user_queue=1` (untried, but what it treats is submit latency, not the limit here, and it needs a kernel parameter change and a reboot). **Not one of them has its premise satisfied by the current counters, so no reboot is requested.**

Gates: this round changed only comments and documentation, and the numerics path not one byte; `LDS_BK` has been reverted to 64 and a rebuild confirms it. `scratchpad/ps_fp4`, `ps_bk32` (pipeline stats).

0bo. **The reboot recovered the wedged USB4 controller, and the two-drive basis is restored; measured along the way: at 17K on two drives the disk is no longer the critical path, the GPU is.** (user 2026-09-30 "let's reboot")

**The drive**: the means tried and failed in §6 risk 20 — PCI rescan, unbind + re-probe of the thunderbolt driver, switching to the other USB4 port — **one reboot made all of that moot, and the drive came back clean**: `nvme1n1p2` / `deepmoe2` mounted read-only at `/mnt/deepmoe2`, `0-2: Ugreen Storage Device` authorised, `rx_speed`/`tx_speed` 20.0 Gb/s each (**these are per-lane values**, `rx_lanes=2` → 40 at full speed, not a downgrade; I misread it at first glance, recorded here so the next person does not repeat it), probe **3.70 GB/s** (3.72 before the dropout, so no degradation). This at the same time **rules out the enclosure and the cable in reverse**: the same enclosure on the same cable now works at full speed, so the "disconnects about 11 seconds after connecting, enumerates as USB mass storage `sda`" seen after switching ports is not an independent hardware fault, it is **a side effect of the wedged state** — the old D2–D4 line of "the bridge is what's broken" does not hold this time. Into §6 risk 20: **thunderbolt configuration accesses timing out across the board = power-cycle is the only option, do not spend time on the kernel side**.

**The three two-drive numbers** (all with margin bit for bit, with a single-drive row for comparison):

| | Two-drive (today) | Single-drive (the §7 0bn run) |
|---|---|---|
| 4K (4,133 tok, ring 2) | **29.80 s** / 139 tok/s, margin 8.598 | 46.78 s |
| 17K (17,010 tok, ring 6) | **42.25 s** / 403 tok/s, margin 10.108 | 56.4 s |
| decode (8-turn conversation) | **9.135 tok/s** / 109.5 ms/token, hit 0.9389 → **ledger #34** | — |

0 errors and 0 failovers on each of the two sources (main drive 118.8 GB @ 4.58 weight, mirror 95.7 GB @ 3.70). 4K at 29.80 sits within the ±3% jitter band of the historical two-drive 29.06 / 29.23 (§7 0aw), and decode at 9.135 also lands on the established basis — **no degradation after the recovery, the two-drive basis can continue to be used**.

**The one thing really worth recording: the critical path at 17K has changed hands.**

```
two drives, 17K: expert io 12.04 s  ~=  expert gpu 11.45 s   (they have crossed)
one drive,  17K: expert io 24.41 s  against expert gpu 12.26 s  (the disk dominates)
```

**I overstated this in the first version, and codex caught it and it has been corrected (see the end of this entry)**: the two buckets being level **does not mean** "the disk is no longer the bottleneck". They are overlapping aggregate buckets, not two serial phases — §3 80 records that under ring 6 **layers 0–19 are GPU-bound, while the reads of layers 20–39 (the replay layers) were always entirely in blind reads**, i.e. read-limited. So the accurate statement is: **savings on the GPU side reach wall time in layers 0–19, and in layers 20–39 can still be covered by the reads**. The ranking has to use `layers[]`'s per-layer times and the per-op timestamps, not the direct difference of these two aggregate numbers.

Even so, the change of direction is real: on a single drive the disk was overwhelming (24.41 against 12.26), and now it is not. This has a systematic effect on the historical verdicts in §3 and §7: **every GPU-side entry that was closed at the time on "hidden behind the disk" or "end-to-end below the ±3% jitter band" has lost the premise of its verdict**, and needs re-review under the new basis (not an automatic reversal — reopening one needs a reason, and the whole point of §7's "not doing" list is to keep closed questions closed). 0bl's patched driver (FP4 GEMM −5~6%) is the most direct one: it did not move wall time at 17K on a single drive, and should partly pay off on two drives, **not yet measured**.

`bench/results/linux/reboot2drive/`.

0bn. **The hardware counter instrument (`VK_KHR_performance_query`) is built, and used to characterise the gap between the LDS GEMM and peak: it is not bandwidth, not instruction count, it is occupancy.** (user 2026-09-30 "do the rest of it properly" + "have CodeX take a look")

**The instrument**: `bench/perf_query.h` (header-only) + `prefill_bench --section coopgeo --perf-counters "<name substring,...>"`, with the extension itself gated by `DEEPMOE_PERF_COUNTERS` (following the pattern of `DEEPMOE_PIPELINE_STATS`, changing not one byte by default). `--perf-counters list` prints the 16 counters the driver has. **Three RADV facts hit along the way, all recorded**: ① counters only answer **on the GENERAL (graphics) queue family** — `radv_perfcounter.c` returns 0 of them for other queues, so when counters are on, `pick_compute_family` has to give up the async compute engine (changing queues is itself a perturbation, so **the times from a counter run cannot be compared against platform numbers; what is usable are the counts and the ratios**); ② **the pass count RADV reports is not a safe criterion** (`radv_get_num_counter_passes` computes from a block's register count, and does not cover SQ's slot sharing): for 13 counters it says 1 pass, and 5 of them come back 0; a group of 4 still loses `VALU Instructions` and `VALU Busy`; **one at a time is the only honest way**, so `DEEPMOE_PERF_GROUP` defaults to 1 (replaying a sub-millisecond kernel once is free); ③ on this chip `VMEM Load Instructions` is always 0, and `VALU Busy` 3.30% / `SALU Busy` 0.39% contradict "already at 56% of peak", so **the two Busy percentages are not accepted**; `GPU active cycles` (2.27M ≈ 0.78 ms, which matches wall time) and `Waves` (2,304, equal to 576 workgroups × 4) are both validated and are trustworthy. There is no stall/wait counter at all — "where did the cycles go" can only be answered by subtraction.

**Attribution**: `coopgeo`'s w1 shape (2304×5120, which is the gate/up shape) at n=1024, system driver, fp16 random weights (coopgeo does not take the FP4 path, so this is this kernel family's behaviour on this shape, not exact numbers for the fused FP4 variant). The data needed totals 34.1 MB (W 23.6 + X 10.5):

| wm,wn | ms | VRAM read | amplification | L2 hit | VALU instructions | LDS instructions | VGPR | LDS/wg | spills |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1,1 | 1.372 | 668 MB | 19.6× | 43.7% | 10.71M | 10.60M | — | — | — |
| 2,2 | 1.089 | 280 MB | 8.2× | 51.9% | 5.37M | 5.44M | 240 | 18,432 | 0 |
| **2,4** | **0.811** | 157 MB | 4.6× | 56.6% | 5.48M | 4.01M | **256** | 27,648 | 0 |
| 4,2 | 0.880 | 159 MB | 4.7× | 57.0% | 5.87M | 3.73M | 256 | 27,648 | 0 |
| 4,4 | 1.149 | **90 MB** | **2.6×** | 62.3% | 4.22M | 2.95M | 256 | 36,864 | **14 VGPR / 1,792 B scratch** |

**The range of applicability of this passage, the two restrictions codex found (2026-09-30, read them together with it)**: ① this run was **fp16 random weights, n=1024**, whereas the real routed path goes through `LdsWFp4`, with each expert's n varying and often much smaller — "occupancy is FP4's limit" is at present **a strong lead, not proven**, and needs re-checking with the real FP4 path at representative n; ② **the `wm2wn4` cell is not reachable on the platform at all**: in `pf_lds_geo` (`prefill_kernels.cpp:264`), `wn = n <= 32 ? 1 : 2`, so **wn never exceeds 2**, and 2304 rows take **wm4wn2** when n > 64. So the row in the table below that really describes platform behaviour is the wm4wn2 one (0.880 ms, 159 MB, 27,648 B LDS, 256 VGPR, no spills) — **the occupancy conclusion (2 workgroups/CU = **4** waves/SIMD, VGPR at the ceiling) holds for it**, but "wm2wn4 is the winner" is a microbenchmark matter only, and cashing it in would first require opening wn4 up for FP4 (at the cost that a 128-token tile computes wastefully for experts with small n, which is probably why wn was capped at 2 in the first place).

**Traffic falls monotonically with the tile (668 → 90 MB) while the time bottoms out at wm2wn4 and then gets slower — so it is not bandwidth-limited**. The winning cell: 24.16 GFLOP / 0.811 ms = **29.8 TFLOP/s = 56% of the mma peak (52.8)**; L2 request traffic 157 MB / 0.811 ms = 194 GB/s, which at a 56.6% hit rate discounts to DRAM ≈ 68 MB = **84 GB/s, only a third of the 236 GB/s ceiling**. The limit is **occupancy**: 27,648 B LDS/workgroup, and a CU's 64 KB fits only 2 workgroups = 8 waves/CU = **4 waves/SIMD** (this originally read 2, off by a factor of two; §7 0br), while VGPR sits exactly at the 256 ceiling (as read by `DEEPMOE_PIPELINE_STATS`). Enlarging the tile further would still save 1.7× of traffic, but wm4wn4 starts spilling registers. This **neither contradicts nor supports** §3 84's "occupancy is not this kernel's limit" — 84 measured going down to wm2 (a smaller tile), here it is going up, and both sides are slower, which says wm2wn4 is the local optimum at this LDS usage.

**So the next lever now has a name** (both belong to the "kernel-family-level restructuring" §3 84 spoke of, not knobs): ① under the wm2wn4 geometry, squeeze LDS/workgroup below 21,845 B (64 KB / 3) in exchange for 3 waves/SIMD; ② or cut the accumulator's VGPR count so that a bigger tile no longer spills. **Measure before doing**: as usual, halve the predicted gain first.

**codex review** (same round, reading `perf_query.h` + `device.{h,cpp}` + the bench changes) reports two things, both real, both fixed: ① `perf_counters` requested `performanceCounterQueryPools` on the basis of the extension name alone — when the extension is present but the feature is not, device creation fails; changed to really ask via `vkGetPhysicalDeviceFeatures2`; ② the profiling lock was released while the command buffer was still in the executable state (the spec requires the lock to cover recording/executable/pending throughout); changed so that `Unlock`'s destructor does `vkResetCommandBuffer` first and then releases the lock, with the error paths covered too. It had no findings on the query protocol, lifetimes, "no behaviour change by default", or "replay is idempotent".

**Gates**: CPU 25/25, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, `gpu_prefill.longctx` first token MATCH + free-running 8/8, 4K `prefill_bench` margin **8.598 bit for bit** (46.78 s, single drive).

0bm. **b128 measured on the decode side (0bl's patched driver, same-session A/B, two microbenchmarks): the MoE dispatches did not change at all, the attention coopmat kernel −28%, the whole layer −2.3%.** `kernel_bench --quick --layer-cycle 8 --iters 48` (the two dispatches of the FP4 MoE, the same yardstick §3 54 used): M=1 per pair **0.624 → 0.624 ms**, M=6 0.692 → 0.689 — identical cell by cell; it is DRAM bandwidth-limited and LDS issue is not its limit (this also explains why score / P.V at 17K in 0bl were level). `attn_bench --layers 8 --kv 640`: **`attn_cm`'s five dispatches 32.53 → 23.41 µs (−28%)**, one layer in total 882.76 → 862.88 µs (−2.3%), the P3 path 763.11 → 743.47 (−2.6%), 40 layers + head **36.19 → 35.41 ms/token (−2.2%)**; the other cells (gate.score, compressor, indexer) move back and forth within ±10% noise, with no systematic change. **Discounted to real decode**: −0.78 ms/token on the compute side ≈ −0.7% of a whole token (44 of the 108 ms is waiting on the disk) — **below the ±3% jitter band, not measurable end to end**, so the value of this entry is in knowing which kernel the money is in, not in tok/s. Of decode's 28.5 ms attention bucket, the coopmat part is now a quarter cheaper. `mesab128/kb_{stock,b128}.csv`, `mesab128/attn_ab.out`.

0bl. **The first cut of driver-level customisation (user 2026-09-30 "the model is fixed, the hardware is fixed … do the most custom development possible"): the local Mesa 26.2.3 RADV lifts the 64-bit ceiling on LDS *loads*, `ds_load_b128` appears for the first time, FP4 GEMM −5~6%.** §3 71 said back then "96 b64 instructions is this kernel's floor, b128 is not available from the Vulkan side" — true, but the driver is ours. There are three gates on the ceiling in total: ① the splitting pass in `ac_nir_lower_mem_access_bit_sizes.c` (loads cut to 8 bytes, stores allowed 16); ② the `/* 96-bit and 128-bit LDS loads are slow */` at the end of `ac_nir.c`'s `ac_nir_mem_vectorize_callback` — **that is the real author** (coopmat lowering emits scalar reads, and when the vectoriser merges them into 4×f16 this holds it down); ③ ACO's `visit_load_shared` already supported b128, no change needed. Both places are lifted under `RADV_LDS_LOAD_B128` (on by default in the local build, =0 restores the original), the patch is in `tools/mesa/lds-load-b128.patch`, the build products are `~/deepmoe-mesa/libvulkan_radeon.so` + `radeon_icd_b128.json`, **opt-in**: `VK_DRIVER_FILES=~/deepmoe-mesa/radeon_icd_b128.json`, with not one byte of the system Mesa touched. **ISA**: all 2,496 LDS reads of the WMMA kernel are `ds_read_b128` and b64 goes to zero (it used to be 96 b64 per wave per slice). **Numbers** (single drive, aligned with the drift of the 0bj run): 17K gate/up fp4 6,385 → **6,027 ms (−5.6%)**, down 2,926 → **2,770 (−5.3%)**, shared 1,492 → 1,438, routed GPU 11.96 → **11.48 s**; score/P.V level (they are not stuck on LDS reads); wall time hidden behind the disk (56.2 against 56.4). **The statement "128-bit LDS loads are slow" is the opposite of true for this shape on RDNA3.5.****Gates (all run under the patched driver)**: margins 8.598 / 10.108 bit for bit, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, `suite.decode` passes, `l3_ppl` off **0.622784 bit for bit** (it affects code generation for every kernel, so it only counts once the decode gate passes too). **Not switched by default**: serve / the web UI still use the system driver, and using it means setting the env above — whether to make it the default and whether to send an MR upstream to Mesa is the owner's call. After a reboot `~/deepmoe-mesa` is still there; the source tree in /tmp is lost (rebuild with the three commands at the top of the patch). `prefill_ahead/mesab128_{4133,r6_17010}`.

0bk. **codex review (user 2026-09-30 "have CodeX review the code together with you", reviewing this round from 0aw on) reported two things, both verified as real, both fixed.** ① The `io_uring` backend's `submit_locked` did a `store_release` to publish the SQE before calling `io_uring_enter`, and when enter failed it threw the error upward and `push_locked` left the slot on the free list — but the published SQE is still in the ring, and the next enter will hand it to the kernel: `user_data` pointing at an already-reused record, DMA landing in an already-reused destination, exactly the §6 18 family (the same illness at both retry points). The fix is cleaner than the one the report suggested: **published means in flight** — `submit_locked` is only allowed to fail before publishing (ring full), enter retries EINTR indefinitely and yields and retries 1,024 times on EAGAIN/EBUSY, and if it still will not move the entry stays in the ring (every enter now flushes the whole batch, so the next push takes it along naturally); the state "failed after publishing" does not exist. ② The engram async submit thread: when `std::async` throws `system_error` it degrades to submitting synchronously, and the exception rethrown by `get()` is converted into a Result — previously both places would let an exception pass through prefill's error path. The trigger conditions are both extremely rare (enter erroring out / the thread failing to be created), and the numbers do not move: 4K 46.6 s, margin 8.598 bit for bit, CPU gate 25/25, `suite.io` passes. codex had no findings on the other seven review points (the LdsWFp4 offsets, the prestaged guard, the single-pass softmax, act_quant's tail block, GpuMon, the op_zero barrier). `prefill_ahead/iofix2_4133`.

0bj. **The next layer's blind reads are issued as this layer's MoE wraps up (`PrefillConfig::read_ahead_tail`, on by default; `DEEPMOE_PF_AHEAD_TAIL=0` turns it off in the bench): the moment `run_moe` hands transit back, `read_ahead(L+1)` is issued, where it used to wait for the next layer's serial phase (cites the first lever prefill_model ranked after §7 0bi, "expert reads during the serial phase 9.6 s").** Measured (single drive): 4K 47.3 → **46.7 s** (the MoE phase's disk time 42.6 → 41.3 s, at the same 4.4 GB/s; P0 queue wait 318 → 168 ms), 17K ring 6 53.2 → 56.4 — the disk was 7% slower overall on that run (serial phase 3.8 → 3.5, MoE phase 4.1 → 3.8 GB/s) and every GPU op was +4% (the run-to-run drift seen in 0bc), while the mechanism's own signature is there (P0 queueing 404 → **229 ms**, the "after" phase 0.4 → 3.5 GB/s). **The predicted −4.8 s did not materialise, and the reason is worth recording**: the model counted "the disk's rate is insufficient" as "the disk is idle" — the disk in the serial phase is already running at 3.8 GB/s (engram's 120 ten-thousand 4 KiB row reads mixed into the stream depress the rate, and pure IOPS alone costs ~4.9 s), so the real gap is far smaller than the 9.6 s it marked. The model's disk floor uses the sequential-stream 4.66 GB/s while the engine actually gets 3.5–4.1, and **the difference is mixed streams and ramp-up, not scheduling**. **A real bug fixed along the way**: the first version did `times_.layers.resize(L+2)` inside `read_ahead(L+1)` at the layer's tail, while `run_layer`'s entry holds a reference to `layers[L]` — a resize across a capacity boundary dangles the reference, and the later `phase()` writes freed heap, crashing 17K twice at random (SIGSEGV/GP, with the core's stack in `run_moe`'s future::get both times). The fix is for `run()` to `layers.resize(num_hidden_layers)` once, after which nobody grows it again. Bit for bit (8.598 / 10.108), `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25. `prefill_ahead/aheadtail_{4133,r6_17010}` (single-drive runs).

0bi. **The verify cost of speculative decoding measured on Linux** (`bench.spec_forward_m_curve`, l3_64, 4,900 slots, warm, single drive) — item 3 said "rerun once MB/token comes down", and decode hit rate has reached 0.94, so measure it once. Sequential decode **69.6 ms/position** (Windows 09-21's `p4_dspark_runtime.md` §8.6 was 96.3), `forward_batch` M=1 79.9 (1.15× slower than sequential), M=2 63.8, M=3 62.9, M=6 **61.2** (Windows 65.2); the verify column P0 = 0, and the union/layer 9.90 … 22.11 are the same as Windows. **Sequential decode got 28% faster, the batch path only 6%** — the decode-chain optimisations of these past days did not reach the batch path. Substituted into §7.4's E[tokens] with the draft free: `chain` k=1 **1.00×**, k=2 0.93×, k=5 **0.72×**; `longest` (not lossless) is best at k=2 with **1.05×**, k=5 0.90×. Adding today's 19–42 ms/cycle draft, all of them are < 1. **NO-GO for the fourth time, and further off than the Windows one** (best cell 1.13× → 1.00×). **When it could become usable, derived backward from the measurements**: `chain` k=5 with a free draft needs M=6 ≤ **44 ms/position**, and **37–41** once today's draft is counted, against 61.2 today — so the batch path has to get 1.5–1.6 times faster (the four items in `p4_dspark_runtime.md` §8.7: the union MoE kernel is 1.6× off its own M=5 champion, one submit+fence round trip per layer, the attention chain, engram row prefetch), or else the decode chain's optimisations have to be moved into the batch path first (that 1.15× at M=1 is exactly the gap). Before that there are three other things not done: there is not a line of the draft chain in the runtime (the kernels are ready), it needs the 7.2 GB mtp expert resident (≈ 390 cache slots, depressing the hit rate), and `forward_batch` is not bit-exact with M=1 (§3 41, at temperature 0 toggling speculation changes the output). Real conversations are NVMe-bound, and the misses the union saves relative to a hot cache measured as 0 (§3 34b), so the disk side will not make up for it. `linux/spec/m_curve_0930.txt`.

0bh. **q's rope (the fp16 output path of `prefill_elem` s5) at 8 elements per thread (two float4 reads, one 16 B write, where it was 4 elements and an 8 B write): 17K `rope m0 f16` 497 → **454 ms**, 4K 124 → 113; bit for bit (10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). The fp32 output path (now used only by blocks of <64 rows) is still at 4. `prefill_ahead/rope8_{r6_17010,4133}` (single-drive runs).
   **17K's current GPU-side account** (the per-op from the single-drive run, ring 6): routed GPU 11.4 s (gate/up fp4 6.15 @ 16.5 TF/s, down 2.82 @ 18, tiled small experts 1.0 @ 1.1 TF/s, scatter 0.59, x16 0.35), attention bucket 12.5 (coop kernels 5.58: score 1.99 @ 6.6 TF/s, P.V 1.47 @ 9, gather 1.18 @ 196 GB/s, softmax 0.42, finish 0.34; dense GEMM 5120×8192 1.77, 8192×4096 1.62, 32768×1280 1.33, 1280×5120 0.26; rope 0.45; index score 0.50), mHC 1.89 (s2 0.68, s4 0.67, 24×20480 0.50), shared 1.53, engram 0.66, host 0.21. The GEMMs are all at 30–45% of the 52.8 TF/s peak (0ag's diagnosis: 256 VGPR, LDS can only read b64, register moves), which is the largest remaining block, but it is work inside the kernel.

0bg. **act_quant (`prefill_elem` s0, also used by MoE's expert quant and the tiled path) changed to four lanes per block of 32 and 8 elements per lane (two 16 B reads, one 16 B write; the block's amax takes a max over two lane exchanges — order-independent, and the scale is bit-identical): 17K `prefill_elem s0` 373 → **297 ms**, `moe quant` 138 → 93; 4K 99 → 79 / 30 → 20; bit for bit (10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). 0bf. **The attention softmax (`prefill_attn` s4) in one pass: each lane's entries (at most 8 float4) and validity bits stay in registers across the max and exp stages, and S and the index list are read only once. 17K 473 → **417 ms**, 4K 115 → 103; bit for bit (the summation order is untouched; 10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). `prefill_ahead/smx1_{r6_17010,4133}` (single-drive runs).

0be. **wo_a's x16 staging (fp32 → fp16, with inverse RoPE, written as [groups][n32][K]) folded into attention's finish stage (`prefill_attn` s5's `kFlagOutF16`): finish used to round O × inv to bf16 and store it back to the fp32 plane, and the staging then read it again to do the rope and write fp16; now finish computes directly the value the staging would have computed and writes it into x16, and the fp32 plane is no longer written (it has no other reader). `op_attention` tells `op_gemm_coop` to skip the staging via `PfStageRope::prestaged`; the folding is only done when wo_a will take the grouped LDS path (`op_gemm`'s coop predicate + the `fused` condition, duplicated), and when it does not, finish behaves as before and a guard was added on `op_gemm`'s non-coop path (prestaged reaching there is an error — the first version had no such guard, so 4K's last block of fewer than 64 rows went through stage 0 and read the unwritten fp32 plane, margin 0.146, caught by 4K's bit-exact gate; `gpu_prefill` 7/7 did not catch it, as its blocks are all big enough). 17K: `attn finish` 507 → **336 ms**, `gemm 8192x4096 x16` 465 → 0 (coop op 2,199 → 1,615), the attention bucket 13.3 → **12.66 s**; 4K attention 3.53 → 3.30; bit for bit (10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). Single-drive runs (the mirror drive is not back yet): 17K ring 6 52.5, 4K 47.1. `prefill_ahead/ofold2_{r6_17010,4133}`, `ofold_4133` (the wrong run).

0bd. **The host bucket broken down to the layer: `layers[]` gains a `host_ms` item (the layer's wall time minus the named ops within the layer), and 17K shows at a glance: layers 0–19 at ~20 ms each (the CPU memset of y before MoE — 280 MB for one layer at 17K), layer 1 at 260 ms plus 260 ms before layer 0 (the engram rows' hashing + sorting + 120 ten-thousand submits on the main thread). Both were moved off: y is done by the GPU in its own submit with `vkCmdFillBuffer` (`op_zero`, 41 ms for the whole 17K run), and the engram rows' submission is on a `std::async` thread, joined before `engram_rows` takes the rows. 17K host bucket 878 → **186 ms** (on that single-drive run), 4K 219 → 71; bit for bit (10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). **The instrument's limits**: `host_ms` is only meaningful in layers whose ops are serial — in 4K's routed phase it comes out negative (some op's timing is longer than the layer's wall time, not investigated), and 17K's layer 1 is now −62 as well (the async submission overlaps other ops). **The accident of this round**: the mirror drive (J.ZAO, a USB4 enclosure) ran for ~90 minutes and then fell off the bus at 74–75 °C (the old problem recorded in §6 and in memory); the run it fell off in was the 4K one: 955 requests err → failover to the internal drive, `DROPPED`, and prefill was **still bit-exact** (8.598, 47.1 s single drive) — the first time failover under a real drive dropout was seen on the machine, and it passed; `echo 1 > /sys/bus/pci/rescan` did not bring it back, it has to be replugged. The numbers after that are single-drive: 17K ring 6 52.8 s (expert io 21.4), 4K 47.2 (single drive at the time of 0ag was 48.7 / 69.75). The two-drive basis will be filled in when the drive comes back. `prefill_ahead/hostlayer_r6_17010` (the instrument), `hostfix1_r6_17010`, `hostfix1_4133`, `hostfix_4133` (the run that dropped the drive).

0bc. **The routed expert's gate and up merged into one dispatch (`prefill_gemm_lds` LdsWFp4's `part = 3`: a row block past R reads w3, and y's token offset adds `up_off`): one fewer drain per expert, and twice as many workgroups in flight (at 17K one expert's gate/up used to have only ~80 workgroups on 40 CUs, two waves per SIMD). 17K `gate/up fp4` 6,698 → **6,226 ms** (−7%, dispatches 13,878 → 6,939), 4K 2,125 → 2,018; bit for bit (10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). On this run 17K wall time was 40.84 against 40.57: every other GPU op was 5% slower (attention 13.4 → 14.1, mhc and shared along with it), GPU temperature 52 °C and the clock reading 600 MHz — the drift of running continuously for an hour, recorded per op. 4K `prefill_bench` **28.66 s** (routed GPU 5.27 → 4.79, expert io 18.3 — the disk is hot). `prefill_ahead/gumerge_r6_17010`, `gumerge_4133`.

0bb. **A pitfall introduced by 0az is patched: after the GPU zeroes them, the transit ring's pages have never been mapped on the CPU side, so the NVMe DMA landing in them the first time faults them in page by page — ring 6 at 17K went 1.0 → 3.1 s for layer 0's MoE, with the blind reads' 0.5 s landing only 0.8 GB (2.4 before). `create` now memsets each transit segment on the CPU (6 segments, 7.2 GB, 173 ms, six threads), while the rest of the workspace is still zeroed by the GPU. 17K `prefill_bench` ring 6 43.2–43.8 → **40.57 s** (layer 0's MoE 965 ms, engram io 1.40), ring 2 44.1 (expert io 12.6 on this run, the disk is hot); bit for bit (10.108 / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). serve at 17K: ring 2 with 4,900 slots **45.0 s** (expert io 12.3 — the mirror drive at 71–74 °C, 10.9 when it is cool), ring 6 with 4,600 slots **43.3 s** (expert io 8.7, other 2.5: 4,600 slots + a 7.2 GB ring is still close to the ceiling, §6 19); the decoded tokens of both runs are identical one by one to before. **How it was found**: when re-measuring ring 6 after 0ba, layer 0's MoE at 3.1 s jumped out of `layers[]`; the first suspicion was that the engram row reads 0az moved ahead of embed (120 ten-thousand 4 KiB reads) were stealing the disk, but `DEEPMOE_IO_BG_THROTTLE_P2=1` (only 1 engram-class request in flight while P0 is active) did nothing, and turning off the early engram submission (`DEEPMOE_PF_ENGRAM_AHEAD=0`) still left layer 0 at 3.1 s — not it; the only other thing 0az changed was the zeroing method. **An instrument added along the way**: `prefill_bench` prints one more `p0:` line (the P0 request count, the average latency split into queueing / service, how many background chunks were in flight at submission, and the queue depth at submission; this time 17K had 21,898 P0s, 400 ms queueing / 6 ms service, 0% with background — the engram class is never in flight at the same time as P0 at all, because the engine submits by strict priority, and while 512 engram requests are in flight P0's depth ceiling of 24 is already filled by the total in flight, so in practice the two classes take turns having the disk to themselves). **ring 6's account now**: bench −3.5 s, serve −1.7 s (against ring 2), at a cost of 4.8 GB more pinned pages ≈ 260 cache slots; the default is still ring 2, the owner's call. `prefill_ahead/touch_r{6,2}_17010`, `p0_{thr1,thr0,noeng}_r6_17010`, `serve17010_0bb_r{2,6}`.

0ba. **The routed expert's LDS GEMM reads FP4 straight from the job table (`prefill_gemm_lds`'s `LdsWFp4` variant, `PrefillConfig::fuse_fp4`, on by default; `DEEPMOE_PF_FUSE_FP4=0` turns it off in the bench): as the A tile goes into registers it is decoded with exactly the same `f32tof16(ldexp(v, e))` as stage 3, and the three decode dispatches and the fp16 intermediate plane are both saved. 17K `prefill_bench` routed GPU 13.26 → **11.86 s** (decode 2,814 → 111 — only the small experts that take the stage 0 fallback path are left; gate/up 6,421 → 6,671 + 742, down 2,733 → 2,856 + 350), 4K 6.27 → 5.27 (hidden behind the disk); bit for bit (8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). serve at 17K expert gpu 12.8–13.3 → **11.5 s**, with the decoded tokens unchanged; wall time 46.0 is this run's expert io 12.8 (10.9–11.9 on the previous runs, the mirror drive). **The account**: the fp4 reads themselves are not slower than the fp16 plane (an isolation run with the decode replaced by a constant: gate/up 6,181, down 2,661, even a little faster than the fp16 path — 4 times less traffic); what is expensive is the decode's ALU (+0.7 s): every token block's workgroup has to decode the same stretch of W again (4–5 blocks per expert at 17K), whereas the decode dispatch decoded it only once. **A cheaper version tried**: assembling half's bits with integers (0.5 = 0x3800, then +0x200 per step, with e added into the exponent field; bit-identical when |e| ≤ 13, checked over the whole domain in Python) — it gave gate/up 9.5 s instead: 9 VALU instructions per element against 4, and wmma and VALU share one issue port. **Not done**: packed 16-bit maths (a v_perm lookup producing 4 bytes at once; Slang has no intrinsic). `prefill_ahead/fusefp4_{4133,17010}`, `fusefp4i_17010` (the integer version), `fusefp4stub_17010` (isolation, numbers wrong), `serve17010_0ba`.

0az. **The prefill workspace (17K's 13.7 GB host-visible buffer) is now zeroed by the GPU with `vkCmdFillBuffer` in one command buffer instead of having one core memset write-combined pages: `Prefill::create` 957 → 204 ms (bench; the zeroing itself 921 → 59 ms — an APU's fabric writes GTT an order of magnitude faster); serve's setup 1,181 → 397 ms (create 1,098 → 327). Along the way: serve's setup log is split into runner / create / teardown / seed (runner 0 — the pipelines are built lazily; seed 47); the first engram layer's row reads are issued before embed (bench 17K engram io 1.80 → 1.52 s, serve 2.38 → 1.88). Bit for bit (8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). **Per op** serve at 17K is −0.78 (setup) −0.5 (engram io) +0.19 (embed 467 → 589, gate 248 → 320: the CPU's first-touch page faults moved from the memset to the place of the first write) ≈ −1.1 s; wall time 46.0 → 46.3 is this run's expert io being 1 s more (10.97 → 11.93, the mirror drive at 71 °C). The 16-thread memset tried first only got to 607 ms (29 GB/s, the page faults being the bulk of it), and was reverted. `prefill_ahead/gpuzero_{4133,17010}`, `engpre_17010`, `serve17010_{engpre,setup,0az}`.

0ay. **The engram rows' fp8 × e8m0 → bf16 conversion (n × 24 × 256 elements per engram layer, 1.04 hundred-million at 17K) changed from single-threaded to 16 threads segmented by token, writing straight into the mapped activations (saving a 418 MB intermediate vector and one memcpy): `host: engram convert` 4K 257 → 29 ms, 17K ~1,050 → 105 ms; the host bucket 4K 440 → 203, 17K 1.9–2.0 → 0.78 s; `prefill_bench` 17K 46.6 → **45.3 s**, 4K 29.3 (mirror drive 3.72, expert io 17.4 — the run-to-run difference of the disk, and that 0.23 s of convert is in the op numbers); bit for bit (8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). serve at 17K with 4,900 slots 47.9 → **46.7 s** (other 3,052 → 1,707), the 8 decoded tokens unchanged. **How it was found**: the host bucket is "the total minus each bucket" and had never been broken down; adding one op around this loop showed it taking half the bucket. The rest of host (17K bench 0.78 s, serve 1.7 s + setup 1.16 s) has not been broken down yet. `prefill_ahead/engconv0_4133` (the instrument), `engconv_{4133,17010}`, `serve17010_0ay`.

0ax. **The engram rows' reads issued early, reopened (the path of §3 78; `PrefillConfig::engram_ahead`, on by default, `DEEPMOE_PF_ENGRAM_AHEAD=0` turns it off in the bench): the first engram layer's (layer 1's) 120 ten-thousand 4 KiB row reads are issued before layer 0, and the next engram layer's (layer 14's) rows are issued the moment layer 1 has consumed its own; the row numbers depend only on the prompt, so the reads land while those earlier layers are computing. 17K `prefill_bench` engram io 3.8–4.6 → **1.6 s**, and the total ring 2 48.2 → **46.6**, ring 6 45.3–45.8 → **45.0**; 4K 0.96–1.06 → 0.45–0.47 s, 29.7 → **29.0** (the other two runs at 34.5 / 33.2 are throttled runs with the mirror drive probing at 3.57 / 2.42 GB/s, expert io 22 s, and do not count). Bit for bit: all three 4K runs at 8.598 (6 of the 17 runs back in 78 were wrong), both 17K runs at 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25. serve at 17K (4,900 slots, default configuration) 49.7 → **47.9 s** (engram io 4,500 → 2,329 — 1 s less saved in serve than in the bench: before layer 0 there is still 1.2 s of setup issuing other reads), the 8 decoded tokens identical one by one to 0at, and the hit rate 0.780 unchanged. **Why it can be turned on now**: 78 was reverted at the time only because it was not bit-exact, and the root cause was 0aw's record conflict (an expert's 1 MiB read landing only after several hundred thousand row reads); the code is the `engram_issue` / `engram_rows` split 78 left behind plus 12 lines (issue the first layer at the top of run(), issue the next layer when `engram_rows` has consumed one). **Incidentally**: 0as's resident scale planes are masked by this in the bench (both reach 1.6 s, and resident also occupies 5.7 GB of host pages); in serve, the resident + 4,300 slots path (46.4) would have to be re-measured to know whether the two stack. `prefill_ahead/engahead{1,2,3}_4133`, `engahead_17010`, `engahead_r6_17010`, `serve17010_0ax`.

   **The current snapshot** (two drives, default configuration: ring 2, scales not resident, engram issued early): `prefill_bench` 4K 29.0 s, 17K 45.3 (after 0ay; ring 6 not re-measured); `prefill_bench` 17K ring 6 **40.6 s**; serve at 17K with 4,900 slots ring 2 **45.0 s** (0bb; the mirror drive at 74 °C, expert io 1.4 s more than when it is cool), ring 6 with 4,600 slots **43.3 s**. What is left at 17K: 8 s of pure disk reads in layers 20–39, and layers 0–19 GPU-bound (attention 13.7 + expert gpu 13.3 + mhc 1.9); 4K is all on the disk (expert io 16.9 / 29.0).

0aw. **The io_uring backend's in-flight record table changed from "1,024 entries indexed by chunk id modulo, overwritten unconditionally" to a free list indexed by the SQE's `user_data` (`storage/linux/io_uring.cpp`): a read that lands only after >1,024 new chunks would hand its record over to someone else, another chunk's future would be taken as completed first, and the GPU would read pages that had not landed — this is the root cause of §6 18 (6 of the 17 runs of §3 78's early engram row reads were not bit-exact).** Finding it took §3 80: with the next layer's expert blind reads moved up to be issued during the engram layer's row reads, the same code was wrong three times at 4K on the old backend (margins 8.819 / 8.422), and after the fix all four configurations were bit-exact. The default path is unaffected (records never overlap) and the numbers do not move: 4K `prefill_bench` 29.72 s / 8.598, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, `suite.io` passes, CPU gate 25/25. `prefill_bench`'s `io:` line now prints GB, weight, request count and average latency per source (`srcsplit_4133`: 118 / 94 GB at weights 4.59 / 3.63, averages 202 / 289 ms — the external drive is 40% slower per request). **One thing to record in passing**: the external J.ZAO drive throttles at 70–75 °C (dd sequential 3.0 GB/s, probe 3.6–3.7, lowest seen 2.43 and 2.87), so IO-bound comparisons have to look at the probe numbers in each run's header; the mirror drive being 0.1 GB/s slower in a run is ±0.3 s at 4K. **The path reopened**: §3 78 (engram row reads issued early, 17K engram io 4.6 → 1.75 s, −2.4 s; 4K −1.1) — it was reverted at the time only because it was not bit-exact, and the reason for that is now fixed; it saves more than resident scales (0as) and does not occupy host memory. `prefill_ahead/iofix_4133`.

0av. **transit changed into a ring of R segments (`PrefillConfig::transit_segments`, serve `--transit-ring N`, bench `--ring N`; the default 2 = the original two halves), with the blind-read depth adapting per layer: if the previous layer's blind-read experts were never waited on (wait < 50 ms) and gate used ≥ 90% of them, go one segment deeper, otherwise one shallower. 17K `prefill_bench` 48.2 → **45.3–45.8 s** (expert io 11.5 → 8.3–8.5), 4K unchanged at 29.5 (the depth stops at 2); bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). serve at 17K (ring 6 + engram scales resident): 4,600 slots 48.2, **4,300 slots 46.4 s** (the 0aq snapshot was 51.25); ring 6 alone at 4,600 slots is 49.6 (host grew another 1.1 s, §6 19). **
   **The new instrument speaks first**: `run_layer` reads `/proc/diskstats` (the sector counts of the two NVMes) at three phases, before / during / after MoE, `prefill_bench` prints a `drives:` line, and `--ops-json` carries one `layers[]` entry per layer (including how much was blind-read, how much gate used, and how long it waited). 17K as-is (transit 64): **layers 0–19** read only 2.41 GB in the 0.7 s before MoE (exactly 128 blind reads, done in 0.3 s, after which the disk is idle for 0.4 s), and 4.7 GB in the 0.88 s inside MoE (5.4 GB/s) — 7.2 GB per layer = all 384 experts are needed; **layers 20–39** (replay, 128 rows) take 13 ms before MoE and read 2.5–3.6 GB in 0.3–0.6 s inside MoE (6–8 GB/s, with the GPU barely working). Across the whole run the disk only does 2.5 GB/s (before MoE) and 6.0 (inside MoE). P0 queue depth 24 and 64 are the same (`p0qd*_17010`) — it is not the queue, there is nothing to read.
   **The change**: the ring has R segments, the blind reads fill the first d segments, and a batch first eats the blind-read ones by segment and then walks the ring K at a time (batch i is issued after batch i−2 has finished computing, and a segment is only reused R batches later, so with R ≥ 2 nothing is read into the segment being computed). The depth d is decided by the previous layer: at 17K layers 0–19 rise to 6 within four layers (384 = a whole layer, 4.1–5.0 GB read before MoE, and the MoE phase 0.69 s ≈ the GPU's 0.65); 4K waits already in the first segment (the disk is the bottleneck there to begin with) and stops at 2. **Why "how much was used" alone is not enough**: 4K's gate uses 90%+ of the blind reads (329–381 of them per layer for 4,133 rows), so going by coverage alone it would rise to 6, and the result is 33.1 s — the extra 10% read (188 → 208 GB) is all time in IO-bound 4K; blind reads are only free when the disk is idle, so what matters is whether anything waited. A fixed `--transit 192` (48.9 at the time of 0aq) is 45.5 today, the same as adaptive, but it would break 4K.
   **serve**: a 6-segment ring is 7.2 GB of pinned host pages (2.4 before), and at 4,900 slots the machine is already too close to the ceiling (§6 19), so at 4,600 slots ring 6's host bucket 2.9 → 4.0 s eats half of the saving; cutting further to 4,300 slots + resident scales: 46.4 s, with the decode hit rate 0.780 → 0.742 (8 steps, does not count). **Neither is on by default**: ring 2, scales not resident; getting 46 s takes `--transit-ring 6 --engram-scales-resident --cache-slots 4300` together. The owner's call.
   **What is left at 17K** (45.5 s): 8 s of pure disk reads in layers 20–39 (in the 128-row replay layers only the disk is working and the GPU idles; what is read is ~150 experts per layer, with no earlier phase to hide it behind, unless it is read early inside layers 0–19 — the ring cannot hold it, though the cache slots can in serve); engram row reads 3.8 (1.6 when resident); layers 0–19 are already GPU-bound (pre 0.7 + MoE 0.69). `prefill_ahead/diskphase_17010`, `t192phase_17010`, `p0qd{24,64}_17010`, `ring6_{4133,17010}` (coverage only), `ring6w_{4133,17010}`, `serve17010_ring6_4600`, `serve17010_ring6scales_{4600,4300}`.

0au. **The inverse RoPE of the attention output o folded into wo_a's x16 staging (converted along the way in `prefill_coopmat` stage 2; `PfStageRope`): one fewer read-write pass over this layer's widest activation. 17K `rope m0 inv` 570 → 0 ms (`gemm 8192x4096 x16` 418 → 437, coop op 2,077 → 2,096), 4K 150 → 2; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). The totals 17K 48.24 → 48.20 and 4K 29.59 → 29.51 — on this run every GPU-side op was 1–3% slower (moe gemm +176, decode +75), and the per-op account is −0.52 / −0.15 s. **
   **Why it is possible**: o's rope is in place (an fp32 read of 8 B/element written back), and immediately after, the staging reads fp32 again and writes fp16. rope's pairs are only in the last 64 elements of each head, two adjacent ones, and the staging has exactly 8 consecutive elements per thread and does not cross a head, so `rope_pair` + `maybe_round` move into stage 2 unchanged, followed by `f16x8` — each element passes through the same operations in the same order. The §3 77 attempt deleted the staging and the tiles got 700 ms slower; this time the staging is still there (x still stays in MALL) and what is deleted is the rope pass. Blocks that cannot take the coop path (a tail < the coop threshold) fall back to the original `op_rope`.
   **An instrument added along the way**: rope is now recorded by purpose (`rope m0 f16` is q, `m0 inv` is o, `m1` the window KV, `m2` iq, `m3` cache) — previously the single `prefill_elem s5` number mixed q and o together. q's pass (17K 560 ms, 143 GB/s, 7/8 of it pure copying) stays: its source is wq_b's LDS GEMM, and in the output tile adjacent elements are not in the same lane, so it cannot be folded in. `prefill_ahead/ropetag_4133.jsonl`, `orope_*`.

0at. **mHC's two 24 × 20480 mixing-coefficient GEMMs (`hc_attn_fn` / `hc_ffn_fn`, fp32 weights, fp32 input) take the new `prefill_gemm` stage 4: 17K 1,104 → 521 ms (the mHC bucket 2,406 → 1,835, `prefill_bench` 50.23 → 48.24 s), 4K 267 → 137 (686 → 574); bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25). **
   **Why it was slow**: in stage 0 one workgroup handles 8 rows × 8 tokens, 24 rows is 3 workgroups, and each x block is read once by each of the 24 rows — all going through L2 (2.7 TB per pass at 2.5 TB/s), more than twice this GEMM's DRAM floor (111 GB, 0.47 s). **stage 4**: a workgroup handles all 24 rows for 4 tokens (wave w handles rows w, w+8, w+16, and a lane still handles blocks l, l+32, …), and every 32 blocks one slice puts 4 tokens' x into LDS (16 KB, stored as `[token][float4 component][block]`, so one wave reading 32 consecutive float4s hits no bank conflict), with the next slice read into registers before multiplying this one. Each (row, token) sum is still over the same lane set, in the same order, with the same `WaveActiveSum`, hence bit for bit.
   **The geometries measured** (4K): 8 tokens/wg with a 32 KB slice **316 ms** (even slower than stage 0 — a CU fits only one workgroup, and the barriers of the 20 slices in series are all latency), 4 tokens **137**, 2 tokens 135 (doubling the weights' L2 traffic buys nothing). Take 4. `prefill_ahead/narrow*_4133.jsonl`, `narrow_17010`.
   **The current snapshot** (two drives, engram resident scales off): `prefill_bench` 4K 29.7 s, 17K 48.2 s; serve (4,900 slots) 17K 51.25 → **49.7 s** (mhc 2,233 → 1,694), 4K 31.5 (expert io 18.1 s — the mirror drive was 1.5–3 s slower this hour, 16.6 at the time of 0aq; on the GPU side mhc 653 → 508); `serve17010_0at`, `serve4133_0at`. With resident on: roughly 4K 28.2, 17K 45.2 (0as's −0.5 / −3.0 stacked on top, not run separately). 17K's mHC still has 1.8 s left: pre_norm s2 0.64, post s4 0.64, these two GEMMs 0.52.

0as. **The engram scale planes resident in memory (`--engram-scales-resident` / `RuntimeConfig::engram_scales_resident`, off by default; `prefill_bench` uses `DEEPMOE_PF_ENGRAM_SCALES=1`): two 4 KiB reads per row become one, `prefill_bench` 4K 30.50 → 29.00 s (engram io 975 → 433 ms, IO requests 313,620 → 168,239), 17K 50.23 → 47.23 s (engram io 3,848 → 1,615); bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7, CPU gate 25/25, and serve's 8 decoded tokens identical one by one). serve at 17K: 51.25 → 50.4–51.1 s at 4,900 slots (not achieved), **48.6 s** at 4,600 slots; 4K 29.9 → 29.8 (the IO floor). Whether to turn it on by default is the owner's call. **
   **What it is**: the two layers' scale planes are 2.86 GiB each (384M rows × 8 B), `EngramTables::load_scales` reads them sequentially into a `std::vector` (1.9 s), and a `shared_ptr` hangs off each session's table; `Prefill::engram_issue` and `EngramRunner::fetch` changed the scale rows into an 8-byte memcpy into the original staging slot (prefill no longer pushes a future, decode pushes an empty `std::future` and `land()` skips it by `valid()`), and not a line of the landing and conversion code changed. The engine loads the planes before measuring `available_physical_bytes()`, so the automatic cache budget gives itself ~327 fewer slots.
   **Why serve does not get it at 4,900 slots**: all three runs had engram io 4.9 → 1.1–2.3 s but `other` (host) 3.0 → 5.1–6.0 s, while in the bench host did not change (1.9 → 2.0). The difference is that serve already has 85.8 GiB of cache + 9.2 GiB resident, and adding 5.7 GiB of planes plus 17K's workspace leaves the machine (124 GiB, THP `always`) with only a dozen-odd GB — and the host-side allocations (staging pages, the 139 MB `x` used for conversion, bounce) start waiting on reclaim or on whole pages. Cutting 300 slots (4,600) brings host back to 3.07 s and the total to 48.6 s; the decode hit rate goes 0.780 → 0.768 (only 8 steps, does not count). §6 19. The engram wait jumps between 1.1 and 2.3 s in serve (a steady 1.6 in the bench): the completion path and the bounce copy (211 GB, 19.6 s) share one polling thread.
   **New instrument**: serve can emit a per-op table too (`DEEPMOE_PF_OPS_JSON=FILE`, the same `PrefillTimes::json` as `prefill_bench --ops-json`); `engram reads` counts only the reads actually issued. `prefill_ahead/scales_*.out/.jsonl`, `scales2_4133` (the run where the mirror drive dropped to 9.7 GB/s, 33.87 s, engram io 445, margin unchanged), `serve17010_scales{,_b}`, `serve17010_ops_{base,scales,scales2,scales4600}` (`.ops.jsonl` is serve's per-op), `serve4133_scales`.
   **The current snapshot** (two drives, `prefill_bench`, the option off): 4K 29.8–30.6 s, 17K 50.0 s; on: 4K 29.0, 17K 47.2. serve (4,900 slots, off) 4K 29.95, 17K 51.25; on and at 4,600 slots, 17K 48.6. **The next batch of levers**: (1) blind reads landing directly in cache slots in serve (transit 192's −1.1～2 s without occupying GTT); (2) fusing the gather into the two tile GEMMs (≤ −0.3 s); (3) serve's bounce copy: 211 GB memcpy'd on the polling thread for 19.6 s, and if path A's slots could be O_DIRECT targets directly (io_uring registered buffers, or changing the GTT mapping), 17K's engram waiting and the P0 misses would both settle down — measure the polling thread's occupancy first.

0ar. **mhc_pre_norm (`prefill_elem` s2) changed to one float4 per lane: 17K 739 → 648 ms, 4K 254 → 208; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7).**
   **How bit-exactness is kept**: this stage's two sums have each lane accumulate its own elements first (i, i+32, i+64, …, in order) and then `WaveActiveSum`, and changing it in 0ak to have each lane take four consecutive elements broke bit-exactness. Now a slice is 128 elements, a lane reads four consecutive ones (one float4), and then `WaveShuffle` exchanges the elements back — element 32m+i is in component i%4 of lane 8m+i/4 — so each lane's partial sum is still over the same numbers in the same order. The second pass (the normalisation) is elementwise, with direct float4 reads and writes and the weights read 4 bf16 at a time. It only reaches 113 GB/s: 4 shuffles + 3 selects per plane per slice, and u's writes are still 4 scalar writes per lane (exchanging back the other way would take 16 shuffles, not worth it). `prefill_ahead/prenorm4_*.out/.jsonl`.

0aq. **wo_a's grouped GEMM merged into one dispatch (8 groups × 1,024 rows → 8,192 rows at once, with x16 sliced as [group][n32][K] and the row block working out which group it belongs to itself): 17K tiles 1,597 → 1,384 ms, the whole op 2,146 → 2,021; 4K 409 → 375; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7). Today's serve snapshot (two drives, 4,900 slots): 4K 30.07 → 29.95 s, 17K 54.8 → 51.25 s (0ak–0aq all together).**
   **Why**: one dispatch per group is only 8 × 8 = 64 workgroups, not enough for the GPU's 80 seats (wo_a tiles 15.5 TFLOP/s, wq_b 25.5). Merged into one dispatch it is 512 workgroups. x16's staging got 50 ms slower because it has to write 8 different slices (342 → 392), for a net −125 ms. The §3 77 attempt had rope write the fp16 plane directly and the staging disappear entirely, and the tiles got 700 ms slower instead; this time the staging is still there and merely sliced, and the tiles got faster — so what was slow in 77 was not the layout, it was the copy of x that the staging leaves in MALL. `prefill_ahead/fused_*.out/.jsonl`, `serve4133_0ap/`, `serve17010_0ap/`.
   **The current snapshot** (two drives, `prefill_bench`): 4K 29.8–30.6 s, 17K 50.0 s (`--transit 192` 48.9); serve 4K 29.95, 17K 51.25. Within 17K's 50 s: the routed expert phase's GPU 13.9 s overlapping 11.8 s of waiting on the disk, attention 5.3, dense GEMM 5.1, engram row reads 3.8–4.3 (the IOPS wall), mHC 2.5, rope 1.1. **The next batch of levers by size**: (1) making engram's scale rows (3 GB per layer, 6 GB for two layers) resident in memory would halve the 120 ten-thousand reads (17K −1.9 s, 4K −0.5 s), at the cost of ~320 fewer cache slots in serve (about −0.5 points of hit rate) — the owner's call (0as made it an option); (2) blind reads landing directly in cache slots in serve (transit 192's −1.1～2 s without occupying GTT); (3) mhc_pre_norm at 99 GB/s: float4 reads + `WaveReadLaneAt` to exchange the elements back into each lane's original set, with the reduction order unchanged (about −0.35 s) (0ar did it); (4) decoding the routed expert's fp4 on the spot in the LDS loader and deleting the decode pass (2.97 s) — estimated by instruction count: 64 elements per lane per slice ≈ 512 VALU instructions, comparable to the ~512 cycles of the same slice's 32 WMMA instructions, and on the same VALU pipeline, so the GEMM would get about twice as slow, more than what is saved, **not doing it**; (5) fusing the gather into the two tile GEMMs (reading the f32 KV rows and saving the fp16 G's one write and two reads, ≤ −0.3 s).

0ap. **The engram row reads' queue depth 128 → 512, and the io_uring backend continues short reads: 4K engram io 1,176 → 958 ms (256: 1,032, 1,024: 981), 17K 4,634 → 3,808, `prefill_bench` 17K 50.30 → 50.01 s; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7).**
   **Short reads**: past a queue depth of 128, btrfs's O_DIRECT will answer an 8 KiB read with only one page (`short read: 4096 of 8192`, hit on the first try at QD 512, and seen twice at QD 128 as well), and the whole prefill used to fail right there. The backend now continues the unread part within the same chunk from where it stopped (as long as it advanced page-aligned), accumulating `bytes_moved`; `teardown` prints how many times it continued. `prefill_ahead/engqd*_4133.out`, `engqd512_17010.out`.

0ao. **The engram row reads' error path fixed and the errors carry a reason: on a read failure (`short read: 4096 of 8192`) the early return no longer lets in-flight reads land in already-freed staging pages (the `prefill_bench` segfault at 2026-09-30 02:10 was exactly this: the §3 74 crash inside libc after the mirror read went wrong), the staging area is now a member `EngramAhead` and `drop()` waits for completion before releasing it; expert / engram read failures report with the engine's status attached; `prefill_bench` prints the engine's requests / failed / per-source err / failover on every run, and dumps each layer's `cmp_cache` into `--handoff-dir` along with the rest. The version with the engram row reads issued early was measured and reverted: §3 78, §6 18.**

0an. **The dense weights' fp16 decode stays in 4 slots and is decoded once per layer instead of once per query block: the decode of 17K's three big GEMMs 1,244 → 76 ms (wq_b 414 → 27, wo_b 432 → 26, wo_a 398 → 23), `prefill_bench` 51.63 → 50.30 s; 4K 355 → 76 ms, 32.5 → 31.8; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7).**
   **Why it used to decode per block**: there was only one `b_.w16` and `w16_src_` recorded only one weight, while within a query block (512 rows) wq_b → (indexer wq_b) → wo_a → wo_b take turns, and at 17K a layer has 34 blocks, so each weight was decoded 34 times (700 times × 84 MB = 88 GB for one weight). Now `w16` is `kW16Slots = 4` equally sized slots (`w16_slot`: use the hit if there is one, otherwise the least recently used), and slot 0 is still MoE's transit (the shared expert and the routed jobs invalidate it by writing it) and the sliced engram GEMM's. It occupies 3 × 84 MB = 252 MB more GTT (≈ 13 expert slots).
   **Numbers**: `prefill_ahead/w16x4_*.out/.jsonl`. What is left on the dense side: wo_a's tiles at 15.5 TFLOP/s (8 groups × 1,024 rows, small dispatches), wo_b 19.5, wq_b 25.5.

0am. **q's rope writes fp16 straight into attention's q16 plane and the q16 stage is deleted: 17K attention 5,675 → 5,295 ms (q16 374 → 0), rope (s5) 1,169 → 1,031; 4K attention 1,526 → 1,445; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7). The same trick for o is NO-GO (§3 77).**
   **The change**: `op_rope` gains `out_f16` (only for the unquantised mode 0): the same f32tof16, just written by rope instead of by the q16 stage, hence bit-identical; `op_attention` gains `q16`, `attn_coop_ok` lifts "will it take the coopmat path" into a predicate, and rope and attention choose the plane by that same predicate (the legacy path still takes f32).
   **Numbers**: 17K reads and writes 72 GB less (the q16 stage's 4 + 2 bytes/element plus the 2 bytes rope no longer writes), and wall time 52.15 → 51.63 s (disk 4.42/3.72 GB/s). `prefill_ahead/q16_*.out/.jsonl`.

0al. **The attention softmax (`prefill_attn` s4) changed to 4 consecutive entries per lane: 17K 673 → 441 ms, 4K 167 → 115; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108, `gpu_prefill` 7/7).**
   **The change**: a wave still handles one (query, head) row, and each lane reads one float4 of S and writes 8 bytes of P (it used to be 32 entries at a time with one 4-byte read and one 2-byte write per lane); the denominator is still summed in entry order — lane j's four come one after another and the number of `WaveReadLaneAt` calls is unchanged — so P and 1/den are bit-identical to the one-thread version. The index list length g need not be a multiple of 4 (n_idx = min(A, 128) + min(512, G)), and its four entries are still scalar reads (64 heads read the same copy, hitting L1).
   **Numbers**: 17K's 78.4 GB of traffic goes from 116 GB/s to 178; the remaining gap is the two read passes (max first, then exp) and the 8-byte write. 4K's total wall time on these two runs was 31.0 / 33.2 s, the difference being in expert io (17.2 → 19.6 s, the external drive), so the GPU side is recorded per op. `prefill_ahead/smx4_*.out/.jsonl`.
   **Not yet touched**: attn finish (471 ms, 16 scalar reads and writes in one thread), q16 (374), gather (1,049, a 231 GB fp16 copy, already at 220 GB/s).

0ak. **Four elementwise kernels changed to 4 elements per thread: −1.45 s on 17K's GPU side (mhc_post 1,440 → 642 ms, swiglu 593 → 286, scatter 936 → 624, act_quant 386 → 357), 4K −0.37 s; bit-exactness unchanged. In the same round, two ways of changing the GEMM dispatch order, choosing the geometry by round, and splitting the engram rows across two drives all measured as NO-GO (§3 74–76).**
   **How it was found**: 0af's model had long marked `prefill_elem s4` as 2.7 times the DRAM floor (88 GB/s against 236), and after 0ag vectorised it to 16-byte reads and writes it was still 90. The newly added `prefill_bench --section elem` runs s4 on its own (n=4,096 / 17,010): 16 elements per thread 90 GB/s, 8 elements 137–155, **4 elements 207–209**, 32 elements 73 — it is not the access pattern, it is that too many reads in flight per thread (16 elements × 9 planes = 36 16 B reads) hold down the parallelism. s8 swiglu and s9 scatter_add have the same shape and were changed along with it; act_quant's 32 2-byte writes became 4 16-byte writes. mhc_pre_norm (s2, 99 GB/s) was also tried with 4 consecutive elements per lane, but its WaveActiveSum would change the order of each lane's partial sum and no longer be bit-exact, so it was reverted.
   **Incidentally**: rope's (s5) unquantised path (q's and o's rope, row width 12,288) was also changed to 4 elements per thread: 17K 1,280 → 1,087 ms; the three quantised paths are still one block of 32 per thread (the in-block amax needs a reduction) and were not touched.
   **Verification**: `gpu_prefill` 7/7, margins 8.598 / 10.108, CPU gate 25/25; `prefill_ahead/elem4_*.out`. The total wall time is within the disk's noise (the external drive was at 74 °C that day and 4K's expert io swung between 15.9 and 17.2 s), so it is recorded per op.
   **The lessons of this round** (§3 74–76): the full barrier after each dispatch in the routed expert chain is not where the loss is — the chain lives on the L2 locality of adjacent producer/consumer, and grouping them or letting decode run in parallel is 40–50% slower; the single-dispatch `coopgeo` numbers cannot be used to fix the geometry of small dispatches in a stream; and the mirror drive cannot answer a 4 KiB random stream.

0aj. **prefill's blind reads scaled up to 64 slots per half, and the P0 queue depth during prefill 8 → 24: two-drive `prefill_bench` 4K 31.7 → 29.8 s (−6%), 17K 54.0 → 52.6; serve 4K 36.1 → 30.1 s, 17K 55.6 → 54.8 (automatic budget, 4,900 slots); bit-exactness unchanged. The "wall" recorded in §3 70 is the P0 queue depth, not the path A landing path — that entry is corrected.**
   **How it was measured**: prefill gained IO tracing for the two phases of each layer (serial phase / routed phase) (the difference of `IoEngine::stats().bytes_completed`, a temporary instrument, recorded in `bench/results/linux/prefill_ahead/iot*.err`):
   - transit 32, P0 QD 8 (the old default): the serial phase's 245 ms lands only 1.2 GB (that is all 64 blind reads amount to, after which the disk sits idle), and the routed phase 7.1 GB/s.
   - transit 112, QD 8: the serial phase 1.75 GB / 7.1 GB/s, routed 7.2 GB/s, and each layer's total duration unchanged (4K 32.9 s) — both drives stay at 7.2 GB/s throughout, while `io_dst_bench` running the same 1 MiB striped reads on its own gets **8.0** (`burst_small.csv` / `burst_big.csv`: the small ring and the 4 GB ring, ram and path A, all 8.0, so the intermediate memcpy is not the wall; the 38 s of that §3 70 run did not reproduce either). What differs is the queue: 1 MiB × 8 = 8 MiB in flight, 4 per drive.
   - transit 112, QD 24: both phases reach 7.6–7.9 GB/s, 4K **30.0 s**; QD 48: 30.7; transit 64, QD 24: **29.76**; transit 32, QD 24: **35.9** (waiting synchronously on batches of 32 exposes a deep queue's tail latency directly).
   - 17K: transit 112/QD 24 **51.9 s** (the mirror drive only probed 2.93 GB/s that time), transit 64/QD 24 52.6.
   - A layer has 384 experts and 4K uses ~380 of them per layer (7.2 GB); the serial phase's 250 ms at 7.8 GB/s can only cover 1.95 GB ≈ 104 of them, and 64 slots × 2 = 128 is just right; 17K's serial phase of 900 ms could cover 7 GB — a whole layer, but transit's single-allocation ceiling is 4 GiB.
   **What was changed**: `PrefillConfig::transit_slots` 32 → 64 (workspace +1.2 GB, and the automatic budget at 17K context 5,000 → 4,900 slots); `p0_qd` / `p0_inflight_bytes` = 24 / 96 MiB, taking effect through `IoEngine::set_p0_depth` during `Prefill::run` and restored on return. decode stays at 8: `io_dst_bench` on the single-expert shape gives QD 8 / 24 / 48 = 2.94 / 3.22 / 3.06 ms, i.e. 3–9% slower when deeper (`decode_shape_qd.csv`).
   **Incidentally**: transit changed to one allocation per half (a ceiling of 228 slots per half), and whole-layer blind reads were measured once (§3 72: 17K 50.98 s, and the −1.6 s costs 4.8 GB of GTT, so the default is unchanged); blind reads in the replay layers are NO-GO (§3 73). All of 17K's remaining expert io is in the replay layers, and getting at it needs cross-layer pipelining + landing in cache slots on the serve side, which is on the TODO list.
   **decode untouched**: ledger #33 (`ahead64-p0qd24-dual-8turns`, `prefill_ahead/chat8`) **9.157 tok/s, hit 0.9389**, which against #32's 9.166 / 0.9405 is −0.1%; the prefill phase 45.6 → 46.2 s (a conversation's short prompts are not enough for the blind reads, and the excess is the share of 5,400 → 5,300 slots at 4,096 context).
   **Verification**: `gpu_prefill` 7/7 (including `read_ahead` and longctx), margins 8.598 / 10.108 unchanged bit for bit, CPU gate 25/25, `run_all` 30/30; serve records in `prefill_ahead/serve4133`, `serve17010`.

0ai. **Two small changes to read-ahead, with the numbers within noise (two-drive 4K 32.0 → 31.7 s, 17K 54.6 → 54.0) and bit-exactness unchanged; at the same time the lever "move more expert reads into the serial phase" was measured up against its real wall (§3 70).**
   **What was changed**:
   - The blind reads are issued as **P0** rather than P1: `IoEngine` only does two-drive splitting and striping for P0 / P3, while P1 always went to the main drive alone, still on the background tier's queue depth, and queued P0s cut in front of it — so 0aa's blind reads only ever used one drive. At the start of a layer no other requests are queued, so P0 is the right class.
