   - run_moe no longer waits for all the blind reads at the start of the routed phase: each transit slot's read is waited on only before the batch it is computed in (`Ahead::range`), and when a half is to be refilled, the slots in it that gate did not select are waited on first (`land`). **The first version missed the latter**, and `gpu_prefill.read_ahead` caught it (logits differed with and without blind reads): a slot that was not selected was reused by the next batch's read while it was still landing — prefill_bench's margin being bit-identical never exposed it, and it was the sink's Fill copy that amplified it.
   **The wall measured** (§3 70): transit 112 (reading 224 per layer up front) gives 38–40 s at 4K (against 32 s) in the P1, P0 and lazy-wait versions alike, and 52.4–54.6 at 17K. The reads' total duration at 4K rises from 17.7 s to 32 s: 204 GB at only 6.4 GB/s, against 11.5 at transit 32. It has nothing to do with priority or splitting (the P0 version is equally slow) — the remaining suspicion is that on Linux path A's reads go through an intermediate buffer + a single-threaded memcpy (recorded in the TODO: `io_dst_bench` is 5–10% slower at deep QD, and udmabuf zero-copy is feasible), and a 4 GB burst saturates that single-threaded channel. **The key to this lever is in storage's path A landing path (udmabuf / a multi-threaded intermediate), not in prefill.** Having blind reads land directly in cache slots on the serve side (a temporary stamp in `admit_streamed`) has to wait on it too.
   **Verification**: `gpu_prefill` 8/8 (including `read_ahead`), first-token margin 8.598 / 10.108 unchanged bit for bit.

0ah. **§6 item 17 is fixed: the cache budget is set from the workspace prefill itself reports, and a long prompt no longer loses the device. The 17K prompt gets 5,046 slots under the automatic budget and prefills in 55.6 s (previously 5,500 slots gave a straight "Not enough memory for command submission"); the default 4,096 context goes 5,500 → 5,300 slots.**
   **The problem**: the engine's automatic budget is total GTT minus pinned, 3 GiB of "other" and 3 GiB of slack, and within "other" the 2 GiB given to prefill was a P2-era guess for 4K context; prefill's workspace is allocated by prompt length (that table in `Prefill::create`), about 0.6 MB per token, which at 17K is 10 GiB. Neither side knew about the other, and once the cache had eaten GTT, prefill's submission lost the device.
   **The change**:
   - That table is extracted into `Prefill::plan(cfg, pcfg)`, `create` allocates according to it, and `workspace_bytes(cfg, pcfg)` gives the total — the same arithmetic, so it cannot drift.
   - RADV's budget: "other" comes back down to 1 GiB (KV, decode scratch, logits), then `workspace_bytes` at min(`--max-context`, 16,384) tokens is subtracted, and the startup log prints all four items (`GTT 112.50 GiB less pinned 9.17 GiB, 4.00 GiB fixed, 10.93 GiB prefill workspace at 16384 tokens`). The 16K ceiling is because the web UI's 65,536 would reserve 38 GiB and leave 3,300 slots — measured once (`gtt_sized/serve17k_auto_dual`, the first version).
   - Before `gpu_prefill` starts, it adds up the headroom of the two heaps via `VK_EXT_memory_budget` and compares it against the workspace, and if it is not enough it refuses with the numbers (`GPU prefill of N tokens needs X of workspace, Y left ... a shorter prompt, or --cache-slots lower`) instead of losing the device.
   - `serve` passes `--max-context` into the engine's `RuntimeConfig::max_context` (that field in the engine had always been design §1.2's 65,536, unrelated to what serve actually accepts).
   **The cost**: the automatic slot count at the default 4,096 context goes 5,500 → 5,400 (a 4.1 GiB workspace, of which 1.2 GB is transit and the rest is fixed items such as w16 that do not scale with tokens). One ledger cell was rerun (`gttsized-dual-8turns`, `gtt_sized/chat_default`): **9.166 tok/s, hit 0.9405**, which against #31's 9.294 / 0.9418 is −1.4%, within the ±3% jitter band; by the machine-recorded per-op numbers, stall 41.9 → 43.6 ms and 2.87 → 2.90 ms per miss, a direction consistent with 100 fewer slots. The headline numbers are unchanged.
   **transit 64 measured in passing** (§3 69): within noise, reverted to 32.
   **Verification**: serve at 17K with the automatic budget 5,046 slots and prefill 55.6 s; 4K with the automatic budget 36.1 s; the startup log at 4,096 context 5,370 slots (5,300 after slab rounding); CPU gate, `run_all`, `suite.decode` (see the commit). The numerics path was not touched.

0ag. **A systematic round of changes following 0af's model: `prefill_bench` 17,010 tokens 80.4 → 69.75 s (−13%), 4,133 tokens 50.7 → 48.7 s; bit-exactness unchanged (margins 8.598 / 10.108). The account of each of the four steps, and which of the model's guesses were right and which were wrong.**
   **1. Rounding merged into the GEMM's write-out, removing the copy (model lever 2, guessed right)**: `prefill_gemm_lds` / `prefill_coopmat` stage 0 does `bf16_round` elementwise on the accumulator (`GetLength()` / subscripting) and then writes y directly (`y_stride` = R, with `y_row0` for slices), and `prefill_elem` s10 is no longer dispatched; each plane of y gets `kPfRowSlack` extra rows so the last token block can write whole tiles. dense linears 12.2 → 8.85 s (the copy's 3.07 s is gone). In the same run, **sizing LDS by geometry (lever 1, guessed wrong)**: a spec constant into the `groupshared` array length, a 128-row block 36.9 → 27.6 KB, and the tile rates did not move (32768×1280 1,248 → 1,104, 8192×4096 1,454 → 1,470, 5120×8192 1,483 → 1,417 ms). `RADV_DEBUG=shaderstats` says why: **VGPR 256, 4 waves per SIMD**, occupancy is pinned by registers and LDS was not the limit to begin with. Kept anyway (using less LDS does no harm).
   **2. P.V's G written transposed into LDS (lever 3, guessed wrong twice)**: two k rows per thread assembled into 8 dwords and the A tile going row-major: 4,751 → 4,693 ms; then breaking the 16-way bank conflict by rotating the write order per lane: 4,698. **Neither is it.** `coopgeo` gained `DEEPMOE_PF_LDS_WT=1` (the variant storing W as [K][R], `coopgeo_wt.csv`): in the microbenchmark [K][R] is only 6–17% slower than [R][K] — the kernel itself is fine. In-place diagnosis (4K, timings only): with `LdsWt=0` reading G (wrong results) P.V is 653 ms = score's 641; a 64-row block is 1,469. So it is **the DRAM pattern of reading G**: in the batched dispatch gid.x is the query and gid.y is the row block, with x fastest → all the workgroups in flight at once are in the same row block, reading **the same 256 B column** of each 1 KB G row, landing on the same set of channels, and it stays that way for all 34 slices; score's column offset changes every slice, so it is only momentary. **The change**: the batched grid is switched to row-block-fastest (gid.x the row block, gid.y the query, swapping the `rec` parameters in `prefill_gemm_lds.slang` + `op_attention`): **P.V 4,700 → 1,317 ms (10.1 TFLOP/s, 1.1 times the DRAM floor), score 2,310 → 1,821**; this attention group 9.9 → 5.9 s. 4K: P.V 1,292 → 360.
   **3. 16-byte vectorisation for the elementwise family (lever 4, guessed right)**: rope s5, mhc_post s4, swiglu s8 and scatter s9 changed to `load_f32x4 / store_f32x4`, and rope's fp8 / fp4 packing bytes changed to whole uint4 writes: scatter 2,323 → 918 ms (63 → 160 GB/s), swiglu 1,410 → 574, rope 2,929 → 1,335, mhc_post 2,379 → 1,443. 17K 77.8 → 74.5 s.
   **4. K slice 128** (§3 68): w1 +12%, wq_b level, wo_b slower. Not adopted.
   **The kernel model's measured terms now** (`bench/results/linux/stage_model/lds_gemm_w1_wm4wn2_loop.s`, w1 wm4wn2, per slice per wave in the main loop): 32 `v_wmma_f32_16x16x16_f16`, 96 `ds_load_b64` (the compiler did not merge them into b128: 4 per tile), 12 `ds_store_b128`, 12 `global_load_b128`, **85 register moves** (36 v_swap + 35 v_dual_mov + 14 v_mov, ACO's price at 256 VGPRs), 35 `s_waitcnt`, 2 barriers. The MMA part is ≈ 1,150 cycles and the rest ≈ 400 VALU/LDS instructions — this is where the money that keeps the GEMM at half of peak is, not in occupancy or prefetch. Going further requires making each tile's LDS reads b128 and pushing the register moves down (a different tile allocation, or dropping below wm4wn2's register pressure), which is work inside the kernel, ranked after transit.
   **Verification**: first-token margin 8.598 / 10.108 unchanged bit for bit; `gpu_prefill.stages` gained two checks — that every value of the wkv output has its low 16 bits equal to 0 (the GEMM rounded it itself), and that the same layer's attention is bit-identical with LDS on and off across two runs (E 64 / 128 take LDS, 96 falls back to stage 3 / 4; 0ad's "bit for bit" had previously rested on the margin alone); all three new mutations were caught: the GEMM writing out without rounding, scatter overwriting instead of accumulating, and `LdsWt`'s k row pairs written the wrong way round (the first BAD report was a stale pattern in the mutation table, not a miss — looking only at the summary line with `tail -1` turns a BAD into an escape). `suite.decode` + `decode_longctx`, `l3_ppl` off 0.622784, CPU gate 25/25, `run_all`.
   **The current snapshot** (single drive / two drives): 4K 48.7 / 32.0 s, 17K 69.75 / 54.6 s (`prefill_model/`, `ops_mirror.jsonl`); serve on two drives 4,133 tokens **32.4 s**, 17,010 tokens **54.2 s** (4,400 slots, `stage_model/serve*_dual`). What is left in 17K's model: the serial phase's expert disk reads 26.0 (12.2 on two drives), dense 6.4, attention 5.4, routed GEMM 3.9, elementwise 2.0.

0af. **Modelling (user 2026-09-29 "you could model it first and then optimise systematically"): a GPU timestamp per dispatch, breaking three composite ops down to the kernel level. This entry only measures and changes nothing; the results and the levers it ranks are below, and the changes start at 0ag.**
   **Why break them down**: in 0ae's model, 17K's serial phase was dense linear 12.2 s, routed GEMM 16.6 s, attention 9.6 s, but each op is the sum of four or five dispatches (decode → x16 → tile → copy; routed is eleven steps of decode / x16 / GEMM / swiglu / quant / decode / GEMM / scatter). Excess at the op level cannot say which kernel to change. The `coopgeo` microbenchmark says the LDS GEMM does 24–28 TFLOP/s on its own, and only 7–11 inside the op.
   **How it is measured**: `cmd_open / mark / cmd_close` were used only by attention's six stages; now `op_gemm_coop`, `compute_coop` and the small experts' `compute` `mark` after every dispatch (query pool 64 → 1,024; a routed batch is about 160 dispatches), and `prefill_model.py` hangs the `gpu:` lines under the op they belong to. The timestamps themselves are free: 4K 50.70 s and 17K 80.29 s are the same as the 0ae snapshot. Snapshot in `bench/results/linux/stage_model/` (`model_17010.txt` holds the numbers below).
   **17K once broken down** (ms; rates against the ceilings of mma 52.8 TFLOP/s and DRAM 236 GB/s):
   - routed 16,639: gate/up GEMM 6,707 (17.6 TFLOP/s), down 2,845 (20.7), **decode 2,983 (214 GB/s, right against DRAM)**, scatter 2,323 (**63 GB/s**), swiglu 1,410 (**47 GB/s**), x16 399, quant 144.
   - dense 12,213: tile 4,940 (15.8–23.1 TFLOP/s), **copy 3,070 (41–46 GB/s)**, decode 1,314 (DRAM), x16 434. The copy moves the GEMM's fp32 output from dout to y, rounding to bf16 along the way — a pure moving stage more than half as slow again as the GEMM it serves.
   - attention 9,643: **P.V 4,763 (2.8 TFLOP/s)**, score 2,093 (6.3), gather 996 (DRAM), softmax 633, finish 468, q16 360. The same LDS kernel, and P.V is 2.3 times slower than score.
   - elementwise 6,580: rope s5 2,929 (**62 GB/s**), mhc_post s4 2,379 (**53**), mhc_pre s2 712 (101), mHC's `gemm 24x20480` 1,053 (55).
   - 4K additionally has the small experts' tiled path at 1.27 s (gate/up 872 ms at 1.3 TFLOP/s, down 398) — half of 4K's expert jobs are under 16 rows and take it; 17K has none.
   **The kernel-level explanation** (per 64-wide K slice, one 128 × 64 block): 32 MMAs per wave ≈ 2,300 cycles; LDS reads and writes ≈ 500; the 24 KB global read ≈ 700 cycles — the steady state is MMA-limited. But the next slice's global read takes 1–2 µs (3–6K cycles) to come back, the prefetch is only one slice deep, and what covers it is other workgroups. `groupshared` is declared at the largest geometry (128 × 128) as 36.9 KB, and `maxComputeSharedMemorySize` 65,536 → **each CU fits only one workgroup** (4 waves, one per SIMD), with nobody else to cover. That is the half between the microbenchmark's 24–28, the op's 16–23, and the ceiling of 52.8. P.V being another notch slower is `LdsWt`'s A tile being read from LDS column-major: 8 2-byte reads per lane with a row stride of 68 dwords → a 4-way bank conflict, where row-major is one 16-byte read.
   **The levers the model ranks** (17K, all predictions halved):
   1. Sizing LDS by geometry (a spec constant into the array length): a 64-row block 18 KB → 3 per CU, 128 rows 28 KB → 2; acting on tile 4.9 + gate/up 6.7 + down 2.8 + P.V 4.8 + score 2.1 + shared 2.0 = 23 s, with an MMA floor of 5.7.
   2. Rounding merged into the GEMM's write-out (bf16 elementwise on the accumulator, then writing y directly): removes dense's copy of 3.1 s; bit-identical (rounding then writing = writing then rounding).
   3. P.V's G written transposed into LDS (2 k rows per thread assembled into 8 dwords), with the A tile going row-major: 4.8 → close to score's 2.1.
   4. The elementwise family (besides the copy there are scatter, swiglu, rope, mhc_post and `gemm 24x20480`, 10 s in total against a DRAM floor of 2.7): all still in the old shape of 16 scalars per thread with 64 bytes between lanes, to be changed to 16-byte vectors with lanes consecutive (what a2858f6 did for gather/q16/x16).
   5. decode 3.0 + 1.3 s right against DRAM: the only way is not to write fp16 (decoding fp4 while the GEMM copies into LDS), and the earlier §3 entry "FP4 arithmetic is slower in prefill" was tried on a kernel without LDS. Ranked last.
   **In hindsight** (0ag): 2 and 4 paid off (−3.1, −3.3 s); the mechanisms in 1 and 3 were guessed wrong — occupancy is pinned by VGPR rather than LDS, and what makes P.V slow is the DRAM channels rather than the LDS layout, and after switching to a row-block-fastest grid P.V is −3.4 s. The model's computation of "where it is slow" was right, and "why it is slow" was half a matter of shader stats, the ISA and in-place diagnosis before it could be settled.

0ae. **The indexer's top-k onto the GPU (`prefill_topk.slang`): `prefill_bench` 17,010 tokens 85.8 → 80.4 s (−6%), 4,133 tokens 51.5 → 50.7 s; two drives 70.0 → 64.7 s and 35.2 → 34.85 s; serve on two drives 4,133 tokens 34.4 s, 17,010 tokens 63.8 s (4,400 slots). The results are bit-identical.**
   **The problem**: in the 0ad snapshot, 17K's serial phase had `host: index top-k` at 4.94 s over 107 calls (fourth in the model, all of it host time): for every query in every layer the index scores were read back from the GPU, the host did an nth_element and passed the KV row numbers back; at 4K it is 0.64 s.
   **The change**: one workgroup (256 threads) per query: the scores are turned into order-preserving 32-bit keys (−0 normalised to +0), 4 passes of a 256-bucket radix select (with the buckets' suffix scan done backwards) find the k-th largest key and how many are above it, and then they are compacted in position order (`scan_wg`: WavePrefixSum + the wave totals in LDS), with ties at the k-th score taking only the r earliest positions, exactly as `gpu::Prefill::topk_rows`'s nth_element does; when the visible positions are ≤ k it takes an identity path. The candidate block mask (`[b][nblocks]` bytes, computed once on the host at layer 20) is passed in as a third slot. The scores stay on the GPU, and only layer 20 reads them back once in order to compute the candidate blocks.
   **The result** (17K): host top-k 4.94 s → `prefill_topk s0` 34 ms; `host: index score readback` 82 → 0.5 ms. 4K 0.64 s → 4.7 ms.
   **Verification**:
   - First-token margin bit-identical: 8.598 / 10.108, oracle longctx 4K 8.4880.
   - The new case `gpu_prefill.topk`: five shapes (identity path, tied scores, mask, window, ratio) compared position by position against `topk_rows`, 0 differences; `gpu_prefill`'s seven cases, `suite.decode`, `l3_ppl` off 0.622784, CPU gate 25/25, `run_all` 30/30.
   - Two mutations: taking all of the tied k-th scores instead of the first r; and window dropping the query's own position. Both caught by `gpu_prefill.topk`.
   **The current snapshot** (single drive / two drives): 4K 50.7 / 34.85 s, 17K 80.4 / 64.7 s; serve on two drives 34.4 / 63.8 s. What is left at 17K (the model, single drive): letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 26.3 s (10.5 on two drives, which needs a bigger transit), dense linear 10.0, attention 9.1, elem 4.7, routed GEMM 4.4. The dense, routed and attention groups are all ops composed of several dispatches (decode, x16, tile, copy, one each), and the model only sees the op's total — the next step is to timestamp each dispatch and model at the kernel level first, and only then touch the GEMM.

0ad. **Attention's two tile stages (score, P.V) moved onto `prefill_gemm_lds`: `prefill_bench` 17,010 tokens 97.7 → 85.8 s (−12%), 4,133 tokens 54.6 → 51.5 s; two drives 81.7 → 70.0 s and 38.2 → 35.2 s; serve on two drives 4,133 tokens 34.9 s, 17,010 tokens 69.4 s (4,400 slots). The results are bit-identical.**
   **The problem**: first place at 17K in both snapshots. `prefill_coopmat` stage 3 / 4 has one wave per workgroup and walks G once for each (query, 16 head) pair (640 × 512 of fp16 gathered KV per query), and P.V walks it 8 more times by dim tile: score 1.9 TFLOP/s and P.V 1.1, which is 5.5 / 10 times their DRAM floors.
   **The change**: write them as the kind of GEMM stage 0 is and hand them to 0t's LDS kernel:
   - score: S[h][e] = Σ_d Q[h][d] G[e][d], where G is the "weights" (R = E, K = D), Q's 64 heads are 64 "tokens", and Y's row width is E. The shape already fits.
   - P.V: O[h][d] = Σ_e P[h][e] G[e][d], where the contracted dimension e is G's row, not the contiguous dimension. A specialization constant `LdsWt` is added (W stored as [K][R]): a K slice is 64 k rows of BM consecutive elements each, copied into LDS in that shape, and the A tile is read from LDS column-major. G needs no transpose and no extra plane written.
   - A one-workgroup-per-query grid: `kFlagBatched` — gid.x is the batch index, and W / X / Y each advance by one problem's size. No push field added.
   - Geometry: the 64 heads are exactly one 2 × 32 token block; the row block takes the widest that divides evenly (E and D are both divisible by 128 → 4 row tiles per wave). In the batched case one dispatch has b × the block count workgroups, so `pf_lds_geo`'s occupancy rule does not apply. A 64-row block was tried at one setting: attention 2.87 s against 2.62 s for 128 rows (4K), so 128 was taken.
   - The contraction is still 16 wide and accumulated in order, the same as stage 3 / 4, so both planes are bit-identical. When E is not a multiple of 64 (a short prompt) it falls back to the original stage 3 / 4; `DEEPMOE_PF_LDS=0` falls back the same way.
   **The result** (17K): score 7.2 → 2.1 s (6.3 TFLOP/s, 1.8 times the floor), P.V 12.0 → 4.8 s (4 times); this attention group 21.8 → 9.8 s.
   **A pitfall hit along the way**: the first version filled the W / X slots in stage 3's order (Q in the W slot), so score computed the wrong thing and P.V read the 64 × 640 P as 640 × 512, a VM fault that lost the device. Located by bisecting with the switch, fixed in one line.
   **Verification**:
   - First-token margin bit-identical: 8.598 / 10.108, oracle longctx 4K 8.4880.
   - `gpu_prefill`'s six cases, `suite.decode`, `l3_ppl` off 0.622784, CPU gate 25/25, `run_all` 30/30.
   - Two mutations: `LdsWt`'s A tile read row-major; and the batched X plane not advancing with the batch. Both caught by `gpu_prefill`.
   **The current snapshot** (single drive / two drives): 4K 51.5 / 35.2 s, 17K 85.8 / 70.0 s. What is left at 17K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 26.7 s (10.8 on two drives, which needs a bigger transit), dense linear 10.1, attention 9.3 (P.V still has 3.6 s), host index top-k 5.0, elem 4.7.

0ac. **The eviction policy re-simulated (user 2026-09-29 "it may not have been done well enough before, you could simulate and analyse it"): E1's conclusion holds, and more firmly than it claimed. This lever is closed.**
   **Reproduce first**: the same simulator (`cache_evict_study.py`), the same 27,399-token trace, C=5,100: LRU 0.9073, E1's best `score-ext` 0.9120, Belady 0.9584, matching §3's 47 bit for bit.
   **Where E1 might not have gone far enough**: its heat decays geometrically by how many times an expert itself was touched, not over a time window; an expert that was hot 2,000 tokens ago and unused recently scores the same as one that has just become hot. Measure the information first: over all accesses, for the question "will it be used again within the next 100 tokens", looking only at the most recent use (LRU's basis) gives AUC 0.77, and looking at how many times it was used in the last 50–300 tokens gives 0.83–0.85. An 8-point difference, which looks like something.
   **Turning that signal into an eviction key** (`tools/evict_window_study.py`: rank = last_use + α × the count within the last W tokens, W 30–1000 × α 500–32000, 16 settings): the best is 0.9090 (W=300, α=2000), **lower than E1's 0.9120**, and a large α drops straight to 0.78. A long-decay lifetime count (decay 0.98–1.0, 16 settings) is best at 0.9101. Recorded in `bench/results/cache_evict/window_c5100.json`.
   **Why the overall AUC is high while eviction still does not win** (`tools/evict_tail_info.py`, `tail_info.txt`): eviction picks among the few hundred old keys at the cache's tail, not among all accesses. Sampling 15K of LRU's 604K evictions and looking at the oldest 400 candidates:
   - 40.6% will be used again within 100 tokens (the ones Belady would keep), the victim LRU picks is used again a median of 156 tokens later, and the one Belady would throw out 12,843 tokens later (or never). **The information is there** — that is Belady's +40%.
   - But among the candidates, age's AUC is **0.509** (i.e. random, confirming the mechanism explanation in E1 §12.6 by measurement); the last-30-token count 0.509; the last-300-token count 0.607; the lifetime count **0.636**. Any feature that looks only at history is at most 0.64 at the tail. That is the information ceiling for an online policy, and E1's +2% is the money at that ceiling.
   **The real workload** (`chat_trace_replay.py`: the route.bin of the 8-turn conversation of ledger #31, 2,317 steps, C=5,500, cold start): LRU 0.9274, E1's `score-ext` **0.9254 (lower than LRU)**, Belady 0.9577. On multi-turn conversations E1's policy is negative.
   **Verdict**: the eviction policy is closed and will not be reopened. Belady's 3–5 points are in "who will be routed to in the future", and history features cannot read that out at the tail (AUC ≤ 0.64), which is the other side of the same fact as §3's 35 (predictive prefetch, precision 0.53–0.67). What is left on the decode side is only capacity and the disk.
   **Incidentally**: the 6,000-slot question is settled too — Linux's auto now gives 5,500 slots (96 GB), GTT is 112 GiB, and with 17.7 GB of pinned plus the prefill buffers it is already against the ceiling, so 6,000 slots (105 GB) does not fit; getting it would mean raising `ttm.pages_limit` further and leaving the system under 12 GB, which is not advisable.

0ab. **Two-drive snapshot: the same binary with the external drive as a second read source and striping (`prefill_bench` now supports `DEEPMOE_MODEL_MIRRORS`, main drive 4.60 + external 3.72 GB/s), 4,133 tokens 54.6 → 38.2 s (−30%), 17,010 tokens 97.7 → 81.7 s (−16%), bit-exactness unchanged. The headline numbers are still reported single-drive; this is a second snapshot alongside it (`prefill_model/ops_mirror.jsonl`, `model_mirror.txt`).**
   **Why this one was added**: the user asked why the model does not look at it two-drive. The single-drive snapshot with only the ceiling changed (`--disk-gbs 8.4`) computes the 4K disk floor as 42.5 → 25 s, saving 17.5 s; measured it is 16.4 s, with the routed phase needing 21.2 s of disk against the model's 20.9. The model is accurate on this dimension.
   **The ranking changes on two drives**:
   - 4K 38.2 s = the serial phase 13.8 s + routed 24.4 s (the disk needs 21.2, the GPU needs 7.2). First place is still letting the disk read experts in the serial phase (14.1 s), but in the routed phase the GPU starts to show too (3.2 s), and after that attention at 5.7 s.
   - 17K 81.7 s = 54.8 s + 26.9 s. First place becomes attention at 21.1 s, the serial phase's expert disk reads drop to 11.1 s (the disk got faster and that phase was short to begin with), dense 10.2, routed GPU 5.3.
   - At both lengths the routed phase becomes one where neither the disk nor the GPU is saturated (4K disk 21 / GPU 7 / phase 24; 17K 22 / 16 / 27), so pipelining the expert reads across layers into the serial phase gives a theoretical ceiling of 4K ≈ max(disk 25, GPU 21) seconds.
   **The bench's mirror support**: `Rig::open_mirrors` replicates serve's `configure_io_sources` minus the health gate and the temperature control (a bench runs for a few minutes with someone watching): `DEEPMOE_MODEL_MIRRORS=dir[;dir]`, weights by probing or from `DEEPMOE_MIRROR_WEIGHTS`, striping per IoEngine's default. The mode string carries `mirror x1 4.60 3.72 GB/s striped`, so the snapshot says for itself which one it is.
   **The drive**: the external drive fell off once before the run (the USB4 link was up but the NVMe did not enumerate, `echo 1 > /sys/bus/pci/rescan` brought it back and systemd remounted it read-only per fstab); it started at 56°C and was at 75°C after two runs, without dropping.

0aa. **The routed experts start being read before gate: at the start of a layer, as many experts as transit holds, 2 × 32, are read in shard order, and after gate the dispatch follows the original rules, with whatever has already been read computed in place. `prefill_bench` 4,133 tokens 58.6 → 54.5 s (−7.0%), 17,010 tokens 101.2 → 97.7 s (−3.5%); serve 4,133 tokens 58.2 → 54.0 s (−7.2%, with the same binary and `DEEPMOE_PF_READ_AHEAD=0` as the control), 17,010 tokens 97.1 s. The results are bit-identical.**
   **Why there is no need to guess**: the model's top lever is "the disk sits idle in the serial phase": a layer's experts are not known until gate has been computed. A probe was added first to measure how many experts each layer uses (replay 128 mode, 4K):
   - Layers 0–19 have 4,133 rows each and use 329–381 of the 384 experts; by the time the first 1,024-row query block is computed, about 300 are already known.
   - From layer 20 on, each layer has only the last 128 rows and uses only 130–206, and its serial phase is only a dozen-odd milliseconds, so there is nothing much to overlap.
   Since almost every expert is needed in the first 20 layers, there is no need to wait for gate: at the start of a layer (after engram's rows have been read) they are blind-read in shard order, with a hit rate of about 92%. This is not the same thing as the several predictive prefetch entries rejected in §3 (#23, #35, #51): those were decode, one token at a time, where one wrong read competes for the disk with the reads that are required; here a wrong read only occupies the disk that was idle in the serial phase anyway.
   **The change** (`Prefill::read_ahead`, `PrefillConfig::read_ahead_min_rows` = 1024, 0 = off):
   - When this layer's FFN has ≥ 1024 rows, the first 2 × `transit_slots` experts are read into transit at P1 (Lookahead). transit is empty in the serial phase anyway, so it takes no extra memory. In serve, whatever the cache already has is skipped (`PfExpertSink::cached`).
   - `run_moe` waits for these reads first (`io: experts ahead`) and then `reserve`s by the exact stamp as before:
     - The ones that were read in and then also selected are put in the frontmost batches (one batch per transit half); the rest are read as before, K per batch, alternating between the two halves.
     - Drop: computed straight from transit.
     - Fill: moved from transit into the cache slot by the newly added stage 12 of `prefill_elem` (copying 16-byte blocks by device address) instead of being read from the disk again.
     - Resident: read in vain.
     The cache's admission is exactly as before.
   - The expert-reading code is collected into `read_expert` and the ordering into `shard_order`, shared by `issue` and `read_ahead`.
   **The results**:
   - 4K: all 20 layers × 64 = 1,280 experts (24 GB) are read within the serial phase, and waiting for them cost only 0.9 ms. 57 that were not used were read as well (a useful fraction of 95.5%).
   - serve at 4K before and after: the 31 tokens generated are the same, the decode hit rate is the same (0.802083333), and the handoff statistics are the same (kept 9,540 / dropped 993). In serve most experts take Fill, so the copy path gets heavily exercised.
   - transit's size decides how much can be overlapped (4K, `--transit`, one setting at a time): 32 → 54.5 s; 64 → 51.2 s (−12.7%, with 0.52 s waiting on the read-ahead); 96 → 51.4 s (4.5 s waiting on the read-ahead, which the serial phase can no longer cover). The default stays at 32: 64 would grow transit from 1.2 GB to 2.4 GB, while on Linux at the default 5,500 slots 17K already loses the device (§6 item 17). Once that is fixed (sizing prefill from the GTT headroom), transit goes into the same budget.
   **Verification**:
   - The first token's margin is bit-identical (8.598 / 10.108; in oracle mode longctx 4K reads ahead in every layer, 8.4880).
   - The new test `gpu_prefill.read_ahead`: 64 tokens with read-ahead in every layer; a fake sink issues 4 "cache slots" as Fills and reports every 5th expert as already cached. Compared against the run without read-ahead and without a sink, the logits are bit-identical, with 160 copies in between.
   - Two mutations added: the copy direction reversed; and a read-ahead expert using the neighbouring transit slot. `gpu_prefill` caught both.
   - CPU gate 25/25, `run_all.py` 30/30.
   **The model**: `tools/prefill_model.py` counts `io: experts ahead` in the routed phase; the routed phase's disk floor counts only the reads after gate, while "letting the disk read experts in the serial phase" is still computed over all the reads.
   **The current snapshot**: 4K 54.6 s = the serial phase 13.9 s (of which the disk read ahead for 5.2 s) + routed expert 40.6 s; 17K 97.7 s = 54.7 s + 42.9 s. What can still be won on the critical path:
   - 17K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 26.9 s (needs a bigger transit); attention 21.3 s (P.V tiles 12.0 s, 10 times its floor; score tiles 6.9 s, 5.8 times); dense linear 10.2 s; host index top-k 4.9 s.
   - 4K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 11.6 s, attention 5.6 s, dense linear 2.8 s.

0z. **rmsnorm (`prefill_elem` stage 1) and engram_gate (stage 7) changed from one thread per row to one wave per row: the prefill of 17,010 tokens 105.6 → 101.2 s (−4.2%), 4,133 tokens 59.1 → 58.6 s (`prefill_bench`). The results are not bit-identical, but they are closer to the reference.**
   **The problem**: both stages had one thread walk its whole row serially (rmsnorm: 512 / 1024 / 128 dimensions; engram_gate: 4096 dimensions per (row, stream), computing three sums at once). One wave's read instructions land on 32 different rows. The more rows the worse: s1 per call is 0.91 ms at 4K and 21.7 ms at 17K — 4.1 times the rows and 24 times the time. The model's numbers: 17K s1 1.91 s, 87 times its floor; s7 2.11 s, 55 times.
   **The change**: as with stage 2 (mhc_pre_norm, Track F3), one wave per row (per (row, stream) plane for engram_gate). The 32 lanes read consecutive elements, and the sum of squares and the dot product each use one `WaveActiveSum`; the output pass is computed elementwise, the same as before. The host's grid is multiplied by 32. The note at the top of the file now says: stages that reduce along a row (1, 2, 7) all use one wave per row.
   **The results**:
   - 17K: s1 1.91 → 0.043 s (118 GB/s); s7 2.11 → 0.081 s (112 GB/s).
   - 4K: s1 80 → 19 ms; s7 129 → 20 ms.
   **Why it is not bit-identical, and why it is nonetheless better**: the summation changes from serially adding d terms to 32 partial sums followed by a tree reduction, which is closer to the reference's (torch's) reduction. The margin changed: 4K 8.796 → 8.598, 17K 10.503 → 10.108. Compared against the reference stage by stage:
   - L1 engram_out relL2 1.03e-5 → 4.28e-7.
   - L14 engram_out relL2 6.82e-5 → 7.60e-6.
   - L0 qr |dnorm|/norm 8.2e-5 → 4.8e-8, L0 q 1.1e-4 → 4.3e-6.
   **At the end of the chain**:
   - forty_layers first token: rho 0.954 → 0.951, max|dlogit| 0.84 → 1.01; free generation is still 6/8, diverging at the same near-tied step 6.
   - longctx 4K: first token 77, margin 8.97 → 8.49 (the reference is 8.35), rho 0.931 → 0.949, max|dlogit| 1.50 → 0.66, all 8 steps correct.
   - ctx16k (17,010 tokens): first token 77, rho 0.848 → 0.859, all 8 steps correct.
   **Gates**:
   - `suite.gpu_prefill` and `suite.decode` pass.
   - `l3_ppl` off 0.622784.
   - CPU gate 25/25, `run_all.py` 30/30.
   - Two mutations added: rmsnorm using only one lane's partial sum of squares; and engram_gate's sum of squares of the key not doing the wave reduction. Both caught by `gpu_prefill`.
   **The current snapshot**: 4K 58.6 s = the serial phase 13.5 s + routed expert 45.1 s; 17K 101.2 s = the serial phase 54.3 s + 47.0 s. What can still be won on the critical path:
   - 17K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 30.8 s; attention 20.9 s (P.V tiles 11.8 s, 10 times its floor; score tiles 6.6 s, 5.5 times); dense linear 10.2 s; host index top-k 5.0 s; elem 4.7 s (s5 rope 3.0 s, 3.8 times; s4 mhc_post 2.4 s, 4.4 times).
   - 4K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 16.1 s, attention 5.4 s, dense linear 2.7 s.

0y. **engram's GEMM (25600 × 6144) moved onto the cooperative-matrix path, sliced into 4 by row: the prefill of 17,010 tokens 112.1 → 105.6 s (−5.8%), 4,133 tokens 60.5 → 59.1 s (`prefill_bench`). This entry's results are not bit-identical; the verification is below.**
   **The problem**: every other dense linear with n ≥ 64 first decodes the weights into fp16 in `b_.w16` and then takes the LDS tile GEMM. The fp16 copy of this engram weight is 315 MB, and `b_.w16` is allocated for the largest dense weight at only 84 MB, so it does not fit and it always fell back to the tiled path. The model's numbers: 17K 7.4 s at 1.45 TFLOP/s, 36 times its floor; 4K 1.85 s.
   **The change**: a weight that is not grouped and does not fit in `b_.w16` is cut into equal parts, each an integer number of 128-row blocks. Each slice is decoded in turn (`prefill_gemm` stage 3 already had `row_base`), does its GEMM, and is copied into its own columns of y: `copy_round`'s (`prefill_elem` stage 10) `a0` / `a1` are y's row width and this slice's starting column, and when both are 0 it behaves as before. Once sliced, what remains in `b_.w16` is not a complete weight, so `w16_src_` is cleared.
   **The results**:
   - engram GEMM: 17K 7.38 → 0.76 s (14.2 TFLOP/s); 4K 1.85 → 0.21 s.
   - The time available to save in 17K's dense linear group 16.7 → 10.0 s.
   **Why it is not bit-identical**: the products on both the coop and the tiled path are exact, and the only difference is the order of the fp32 summation. engram's output comes before all the later layers, so the margin moves: 4K 8.268 → 8.796, 17K 9.736 → 10.503.
   **Verification against the reference** (comparing before and after slicing with the same switch):
   - The agreement at the engram_out level is unchanged, L14 relL2 6.774e-5 → 6.817e-5.
   - forty_layers first token: rho 0.941 → 0.954, max|dlogit| 1.254 → 0.839. Free generation 8/8 → 6/8, diverging at step 6; that step is near-tied, with rho about 0.5.
   - longctx 4K: first token 77, margin 8.04 → 8.97 (the reference is 8.35), rho 0.934 → 0.931, all 8 steps correct.
   - longctx ctx16k (17,010 tokens, oracle mode): first token 77 agreeing with the reference, margin 10.14 (the reference is 9.24), rho 0.848, all 8 steps correct.
   **Gates**:
   - `suite.gpu_prefill` and `suite.decode` pass.
   - `l3_ppl` off 0.622784.
   - CPU gate 25/25, `run_all.py` 30/30.
   - One mutation added: every slice copied into the first slice's columns. `gpu_prefill` caught it.
   **The current snapshot**: 4K 59.1 s = the serial phase 13.6 s + routed expert 45.5 s; 17K 105.6 s = the serial phase 58.6 s + 47.0 s. What can still be won on the critical path:
   - 17K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 30.5 s; attention 21.4 s (P.V tiles 11.8 s, 10 times its floor; score tiles 7.2 s, 6 times); dense linear 10.0 s; elem 8.5 s (`s7` engram_gate 2.1 s, 55 times its floor; `s1` rmsnorm 1.9 s, 87 times: both are one thread per row); the host's index top-k 5.1 s.
   - 4K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 16.4 s, attention 5.1 s, dense linear 2.8 s.

0x. **Attention's gather and the two fp32/fp8→fp16 conversion stages changed to 8 consecutive elements per thread with 16-byte reads and writes, with bit-identical results: the prefill of 17,010 tokens 128.8 → 112.1 s (−13%), 4,133 tokens 63.8 → 60.5 s (`prefill_bench`).**
   **The problem**: all three kernels had one thread handle a whole block (gather: a whole 512-dimension row; `prefill_coopmat` stage 1 / 2: one block of 32 elements) and then do 2-byte writes element by element. One wave instruction's reads and writes are scattered over 32 cache lines.
   The model's numbers: 17K's gather 10.5 s (22 GB/s, 10.7 times its own floor), q16 2.7 s (9.5 times).
   **The change**: each thread handles 8 consecutive elements: one or two 16-byte reads, converted one by one with the original formula, then packed into one uint4 for a single 16-byte write (`f16x8`, placed in `prefill_common.slang`, with element i still at byte 2i). One wave instruction covers 512 consecutive bytes. The grid arithmetic is collected into `PrefillRunner::stage_groups`, shared by the five dispatch sites, and `groups_for32` is deleted.
   **17K's results**:
   - gather 10.5 → 1.02 s, 227 GB/s, at the floor.
   - q16 2.7 → 0.36 s.
   - The dense linears and MoE's activation conversion get faster along with them: wo_a (`coop 8192x4096`) 1.29 → 0.78 s (4K), routed expert GPU 18.6 → 16.5 s (17K).
   **Verification**:
   - The first token's margin is bit-identical (8.268 / 9.736).
   - `suite.gpu_prefill` passes.
   - `l3_ppl` off 0.622784.
   - One mutation added: `f16x8` swapping the high and low halfwords of each word. `gpu_prefill` caught it.
   **The current snapshot**: 4K 60.5 s = the serial phase 15.1 s + routed expert 45.4 s; 17K 112.1 s = the serial phase 65.2 s + 46.9 s. What can still be won on the critical path:
   - 17K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 30.4 s, attention 20.8 (P.V tiles 11.8 s, 10 times its floor; score tiles 6.6 s, 5.5 times), dense linear 16.7, elem 8.7, host 5.3.
   - 4K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 17.7 s, attention 5.1, dense linear 4.4.

0w. **The indexer scoring (`prefill_attn` stage 2) rewritten, with bit-identical results: the prefill of 17,010 tokens 147.5 → 128.8 s (−12.7%), 4,133 tokens 65.0 → 63.8 s (`prefill_bench`); this op 17.6 s → 0.48 s / 1.09 s → 0.03 s, rising from 0.21 to 3.9 TFLOP/s.**
   **Where the old kernel was slow**: a workgroup computed only 1 query × 8 keys, yet it had to move this query's whole q (32 heads × 128, 16 KB) and the 8 keys into LDS before computing the 256 dot products. Moreover the 32 lanes read `gPool[h*128 + d]` by head, addresses 128 floats apart, all landing on the same bank, a 32-way conflict. At 17K there are 900 ten-thousand such workgroups.
   **The new formulation**: one workgroup handles (256 keys, 1 query).
   - Each lane handles one head, with q in registers.
   - Keys go into `gPool` 32 at a time, each wave takes 4, and 4 dot-product chains are interleaved; all lanes read the same key word, which is a broadcast, so there is no bank conflict.
   - Each (head, key) dot product is still accumulated in d = 0..127 order and it is still the same `WaveActiveSum` across heads, so the results are bit-identical to the old kernel.
   - Keys outside the completion mask are written as -inf straight away and no longer computed, saving half on average. The cost model's FLOPs are also changed to count only the visible keys.
   - It requires head_dim to be 128 and at most 32 heads (V4.1 is 32 × 128), and reports Unimplemented otherwise.
   **Verification**:
   - The 4K / 17K first-token margins are bit-identical to before the change (8.268 / 9.736).
   - `suite.gpu_prefill` passes (index_score's mask compared item by item, the values against the oracle, longctx 4K).
   - `l3_ppl` off 0.622784.
   - One mutation added: the visible range off by one. `gpu_prefill` caught it.
   **The current model snapshot** (`prefill_model/model.txt`): 4K 63.8 s = the serial phase 18.4 s + routed expert 45.4 s; 17K 128.8 s = the serial phase 81.6 s + 47.1 s. What can still be won on the critical path:
   - 17K: attention 33.1 s, letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 28.5, dense linear 20.3, elem 9.0, host 5.2.
   - 4K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 21.1 s, attention 7.5, dense linear 5.3.

0v. **The engram row reads got their own queue depth (128 on Linux, `IoConfig::engram_qd`, changeable with `DEEPMOE_IO_ENGRAM_QD`): the prefill of 17,010 tokens 159.8 → 147.5 s (−7.7%), 4,133 tokens 68.1 → 65.0 s (`prefill_bench`); the engram reads 18.9 → 4.8 s / 4.5 → 1.1 s, already right against the floor the model gives them. Conversational decode unchanged (ledger engram-qd128-8turns: 7.622 tok/s against #28's 7.608; engram per token 1.88 → 1.81 ms, hit rate bit-identical).**
   **How the model found it**: in 0u's table, 17K's engram reads were 120 ten-thousand 4 KiB reads taking 19 s, only 63K IOPS. After temporary instrumentation it turned out that issuing the requests takes only 0.19 s, and page faults are not the reason either (touching the staging area in advance does not change the time), while the in-flight count of the Engram class inside the engine averages **7.9**.
   The cause is in `IoEngine::tuning_from_env`: the queue depths of P1–P3 were clamped unconditionally to IoConfig's default of 8 (Track Q2's intent was "P0 got deeper, do not let backfill get deeper with it"), and Engram, being P2, was clamped along with them. serve raises `max_inflight_ops` to 24 and Engram still cannot get it.
   `nvme_bench` measured read-only on engram's 101 GB file: QD 8 → 49K, 24 → 119K, 64 → 190K, 128 → 245K.
   **The change**:
   - `IoConfig::engram_qd` separately for the Engram class. Linux's `runtime_shape` sets it to 128, and `widen_for_env` enlarges the ring queue enough to hold it (P0 keeps its explicit 8, and the background classes are still 8).
   - serve's IO shape is extracted into `IoEngine::runtime_shape`, shared by the engine and `prefill_bench`. Until now the bench used the default IoConfig (4 MiB × QD 8), which was not the same thing as serve.
   - 8 / 64 / 128 were each run once at 4K (`bench/results/linux/engram_qd/`, and the 128 one is the current snapshot in `prefill_model/`): engram reads 4.52 / 1.37 / 1.19 s, and per "keep only the best" 128 was taken.
   **The current model snapshot** (`prefill_model/model.txt`, this commit's binary): 4K 65.0 s, the serial phase 19.6 s + routed expert 45.4 s; 17K 147.5 s, the serial phase 100.1 s. What can be won on the critical path, in order:
   - 17K: attention 34.6 s, letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 28.8, dense linear 20.2, indexer 17.7, elem 8.7, host 5.4.
   - 4K: letting the disk read experts in the serial phase 22.2 s, attention 7.5, dense linear 5.3.
   **Incidentally**: when `nvme_bench`'s `--file` points at a file that already exists and that it did not create itself, it is always read-only. Previously a file smaller than `--size-gb` would be overwritten with the test file, and now it is refused outright (verified with a 1 MiB file, contents byte-for-byte unchanged), so it can safely point at checkpoint shards.

0u. **A complete cost model for prefill (`tools/prefill_model.py`, user 2026-09-29 "is there a complete modelling analysis"): every op records its own FLOPs, minimum memory traffic in bytes and disk request count at dispatch time from the real shapes, a floor is computed against the measured hardware ceilings, and then every lever is priced along the critical path. The conclusion: 4K is disk-limited (floor 43.8 s, measured 68.1 s), 17K is compute-limited (measured 159.8 s, with a GPU floor of only 7.8 s and a disk floor of 47.8 s).**
   **How it is recorded**: each item of `PrefillTimes::per_op` changes from `(ms, calls)` to `{ms, flop, bytes, reads, calls}`. Every op computes its own cost from the shapes at the dispatch site (`pf_gemm_cost`, attention's six stages, the indexer, the 11 elem stages, MoE's shared / routed batches, the expert and engram disk reads) and books it through `flush_one` / `mark()` / `add_op`, with nothing computed by hand. `prefill_bench --ops-json` writes one JSON line per run, and `tools/prefill_model.py` reads it.
   "Bytes" here is the amount an op has to move at a minimum: each input read once, each output written once, with intermediates that can stay on chip after fusion not counted. So the whole attention block (`attn (coop, submit+wait)`) is computed as fused: q, the index table and the window KV each read once, and o written once; the six stages under it are each computed from their own inputs and outputs.
   **The ceilings are all measured on this machine** (`model_probe --json`, `bench/results/linux/gpu_model/constants.json`):
   - The matrix unit: the newly added `probe_mma.slang`, with the tiles entirely in registers and 8 fp16 16×16×16 multiply-adds per wave. **52.8 TFLOP/s** (the theoretical value at 2.9 GHz is 59.4). All FLOPs are priced against the matrix unit, including those that run on FMA today (the indexer, the tiled GEMM), so the floor is "where rewriting this op could get to", not "where the existing kernel could get to". fp32 FMA is 18.3 TFLOP/s, for reference only.
   - DRAM: the best cold stream, 236 GB/s.
   - One dispatch (launch + drain): 2.4 µs.
   - The disk: sequential bandwidth taken as prefill's continuous stream at 4.66 GB/s. The IOPS for 4 KiB random reads measured by `nvme_bench` read-only on one engram layer's 101 GB file: 49K at QD 8, 119K at 24, 190K at 64, **245K** at 128 (`prefill_model/nvme_rand4k_span101g.csv`; the same drive on a 4 GB test file gives 93K / 276K / 291K, and once the span grows the DRAM-less drive's 64 MiB HMB mapping cache can no longer cover it).
   **The overall account** (`bench/results/linux/prefill_model/model.txt`, this commit's binary, replay 128):

   | | 4,133 tokens | 17,010 tokens |
   |---|---|---|
   | measured | 68.1 s | 159.8 s |
   | the amount on the GPU | 74.0 TFLOP / 430 GB | 300.7 TFLOP / 1,127 GB |
   | GPU floor (summed per op) | 2.2 s | 7.8 s |
   | the amount on the disk / the floor | 200.6 GB / 43.8 s | 208.7 GB / 47.8 s |
   | critical path: the serial phase (the disk idle) | 22.9 s | 112.5 s |
   | critical path: the routed expert phase (disk ∥ GPU) | 45.2 s (the disk needs 42.8, the GPU 7.8) | 47.3 s (the disk needs 43.7, the GPU 18.6) |

   The 74.0 TFLOP matches the 73.5 TFLOP hand-computed back in §2.5.
   **Why the critical path has to be computed separately**: a layer's routed experts are not known until gate has run, so each layer is split into two phases.
   - **The serial phase**: attention, dense linear, elem, the indexer, engram, the shared expert, host. In this phase the disk has nothing to read, so every 1 s saved is 1 s.
   - **The routed expert phase**: reading and computing are double-buffered, so the duration = max(disk, GPU) + the first batch's fill.
   So the routed expert's GPU excess looks like 6.8 s (4K) / 15.9 s (17K), but what can really be won is only the part of this phase above the disk floor: **2.5 s / 3.6 s**. There is also one lever a per-op table cannot see: the disk sits idle throughout the serial phase.
   **The gains priced along the critical path** (how much the whole run saves when an op reaches its floor, in s, 4K / 17K):

   | lever | 4K | 17K | note |
   |---|---|---|---|
   | band attention | 7.5 | **32.5** | the fused floor is 0.13 / 0.50 s, and the measurement is 61× / 66× that. The six stages are each 5–11× off their own floors too (P.V 23.5 GB/s, gather 22, score 44, q16 25). The G plane [b][640][512] fp16 is 11 GB per layer at 17K: written once, read twice |
   | letting the disk read experts in the serial phase | **25.3** | 28.6 | the premise is knowing which experts this layer needs before gate |
   | dense linear | 5.3 | 20.6 | of which engram's 25600×6144 exceeds the w16 intermediate and still takes the tiled path, 1.8 / 7.4 s, 37× its floor; the other coop linears are only at 4.4–9.4 TFLOP/s over the whole run, while a microbenchmark of the same shape does 24 TFLOP/s, the difference being the fp8→fp16 decode, the activation conversion and one submit+wait per op |
   | indexer scoring (`prefill_attn` s2) | 1.1 | **17.6** | 0.21 TFLOP/s, 250× the floor: scalar FMA, with one workgroup handling 8 keys × 1 query |
   | engram row reads | 3.5 | 14.8 | 120 ten-thousand 4 KiB reads only reach 63K IOPS, below QD 8's 93K. The Engram class uses the background queue depth. And the row numbers depend only on the token id, so they could be issued at the very start of prefill |
   | elem | 1.6 | 8.8 | 17K's rmsnorm (s1) is 91× its floor and engram_gate (s7) 55×: both have one thread walk a whole row, the same illness the softmax had back then |
   | host index top-k | 0.7 | 5.1 | on the CPU |
   | routed expert GPU | 2.5 | 3.6 | see above |
   | shared expert | 0.5 | 1.8 | it is computed before the first batch of expert reads is issued, so it is in the serial phase too |

   Read with the "halve it" rule, the most valuable at 17K are, in order: attention, the indexer, the engram row reads, the engram GEMM, elem; the most valuable at 4K is putting the disk's idle time to use, and after that attention.
   **Fixed in passing**: `nvme_bench`'s random reads had request i and request i+qd share one destination buffer, and since requests complete out of order, the later one could write into the same block while the earlier one was still in flight. btrfs has to verify the data checksum in the user buffer for an O_DIRECT read, hence EIO (the kernel log's `csum failed`, all on this test file's own inode). It is now a free buffer pool returned by the completion callback, which also caps the queue depth. The test file is written buffered too. Every read in the engine has its own destination address and is unaffected; there have been no other btrfs errors since boot.

0t. **prefill's GEMM changed to a tile with four waves sharing LDS (`prefill_gemm_lds.slang`), and attention's softmax changed to one wave per row: serve's prefill of 4,133 tokens 78.8 → 66.2 s (−16%), 17,010 tokens 223.8 → 156.6 s (−30%, cache 4,400 slots), with the output bit-identical. `DEEPMOE_PF_LDS=0` falls back to stage 0.**
   **GEMM**: a workgroup is made of 2×2 waves and handles a block of (32·wm rows) × (32·wn tokens). For each 64-wide K slice, the W rows and the X rows are each copied into LDS once (one 16-byte load at a time, with 1 uint4 of padding left per row), while the next slice is prefetched into registers. Each output tile is still accumulated in the order k = 0, 16, 32, …, so Y is bit-identical to stage 0.
   The geometry is chosen from a `coopgeo` sweep (`prefill4k/coopgeo_lds.csv`): wn = 1 when there are no more than 32 tokens, otherwise 2; wm = 4 when the row count is divisible by 128 and there are still ≥ 32 workgroups left, otherwise 2.
   w1 (2304×5120) at n = 4096 goes 41.4 → 4.0 ms (2.3 → 24 TFLOP/s), and wo_b (5120×8192) 325 → 26 ms.
   Both the dense linears (n ≥ 64) and the experts (n ≥ 16 and a shape that fills out) take it, and a shape that does not fill out goes back to stage 0. Decode and short prefills in a conversation take the tiled path and are unaffected.
   **softmax** (`prefill_attn` stage 4): previously one thread walked a whole row of E entries, taking 6.7 s at 4K. Now one wave handles one (query, head): the max uses `WaveActiveMax`, and the sum is still folded in one by one in entry order, so both P and inv are bit-identical to before. 6.7 → 0.15 s.
   **The breakdown** (serve / prefill_bench buckets):
   - 4K: attention 22.4 → 13.5 s, shared expert 2.3 → 0.54 s, expert GPU 11.7 → 7.2 s.
   - 17K: attention 108.0 → 73.1 s, expert GPU 52.7 → 17.2 s.
   Once 17K computes faster, more of the expert IO is exposed, which is the origin of "the routed expert phase is stuck on the disk" in 0u's model.
   **Verification**:
   - The `route.bin` of both 4K and 17K is bit-identical to before the change.
   - `suite.gpu_prefill`: stages 110/110, forty_layers 8/8, longctx 4K first token 77 + 8/8 steps, and a rerun is byte-for-byte identical.
   - `suite.decode`, `decode_longctx` and `integration` all pass.
   - `l3_ppl` off's NLL 0.622784 (the Linux baseline).
   (`bench/results/linux/prefill4k/`: `base` / `lds` / `lds_softmax` are three 4K serve runs on the same machine, 78.8 / 71.3 / 66.2 s; `p17k_4400_before` / `p17k_4400` are the 17K before and after.)
   **17K loses the device at the default 5,500 slots** (`p17k_new`: the kernel reports "Not enough memory for command submission"). 17K prefill's buffers plus the 96 GiB expert cache exceed GTT, which is a problem that predates this change, so 17K was measured at 4,400 slots. See §6's 17.

0s. **Measurement hygiene: the conversational benchmark on Linux has all along been a "warm start" — at startup serve restores the last stretch of context from the previous run out of the KV cache on disk and replays it into the expert cache. `hitrate_bench` now passes `--no-kv-disk` by default, and the ledger has one more column, `start KV`. The clean-start baselines: single drive 7.608 tok/s (ledger #28), two drives 9.203 (#29).**
   **How it was noticed**: while looking at §7 item 2 (KV prefix reuse) it turned out that `serve` had long had `.pkv` on by default (Track R2) while `hitrate_bench` was not passing `--no-kv-disk`. The Windows-era A/B scripts all passed it explicitly (`d2_abab.py`: "so that cell N's .pkv does not leak into cell N+1"), and it was lost in the move to Linux.
   The result is that of the 41 benchmark runs on Linux so far, 39 restored the `default` session at startup (of the others, 1 was refused because the context was too long and 1 had no `.pkv` on disk yet): mostly 396 tokens (the last turn of a previous `long_turns`), but also 365 / 234 / 162 / 54 depending on which script ran previously, each replaying at most 128 positions. Moreover every exit writes its own session back into `~/.cache/deepmoe/kv/`, overwriting the `default` session the user left behind with `tools/chat.py`.
   **How large the effect is** (the same binary, #28 against #24): only **turn 1** differs, with the hit rate 0.9515 → 0.9394; the hit rates and step counts of turns 2–8 are bit-identical. Overall 7.683 → **7.608 tok/s (−1.0%)**, hit 0.9439 → 0.9425, prefill in total 66.0 → 71.0 s, and **ms/miss 4.66 → 4.63, unchanged**. Two drives lose about 1% likewise: #26 9.302 → #29 **9.203**, ms/miss 2.90 → 2.90.
   **The conclusions are unaffected**: the two arms of each A/B start from the same point (both restoring 396, except #22 which restored 54 and therefore took a little less advantage than its group), and the ms/miss the IO changes are judged by is unaffected by this either. But the absolute values in the README and §1 are about 1% high, and have been replaced by the clean-start #28 / #29.
   **The current practice**: `hitrate_bench` always passes `--no-kv-disk` unless `--serve-arg --kv-dir` names a directory; `perf_report --record` reads the number of tokens restored at startup out of `serve.log` and records it in `chat.kv_restored`, which the ledger table's `start KV` column displays. The existing 20 rows were backfilled from their own `serve.log`s, and the file was checked line by line with no differences other than the new field.

0r. **Striping on by default with a mirror (user 2026-09-29 "you can turn it on"), and a mirror that fell off is picked back up automatically once the drive returns: a second read source is striped by chunk as soon as it is found, and `DEEPMOE_MIRROR_STRIPE=0` turns it off. The default path with only `DEEPMOE_MIRROR_AUTO=1` set (ledger #26) gives 9.302 tok/s, in line with the explicitly enabled #22 (9.291); the headline numbers are still reported single-drive (#24 7.682).**
   In `IoEngine::set_sources`, `stripe_ = mirrors_on_`, so the environment variable now only has the "off" direction; `serve` (which auto-discovers mirrors on Linux) and the web UI (`find_mirrors` passing `--mirror`) both take this path and need no change.
   The unit test `io.stripe_is_the_default_and_leaves_backfill_whole`: on by default when there is a mirror, always off when there is none (not even `set_stripe(true)` helps); one mutation added (the default put back to off), which is caught, io 6/6.
   #26's external drive peaked at 74 °C for the whole session (63–67 idle), the temperature control did not trigger, and there were zero errors; stall 41.7 ms, ms/miss 2.90, prefill 43.2 s.
   (#26 is recorded against `130ff40829`: that is the pre-amend commit, and its code differs from `989a606` only in comments.)
   **Picking back up after a drive drops**: the USB4 link drops about every 1–1.5 hours, systemd remounts the drive a few seconds later per fstab, but the device name changes and fds opened before the disconnect keep returning EIO. A mirror kicked out by SourceHealth used to come back only when the process restarted, so a long-running web engine that dropped once ran single-drive until the restart.
   It now picks back up like this: `File::reopen` reopens the same path with the original open mode (`F_GETFL`, including `O_DIRECT`), checks the length, and then `dup3`s it onto **the same fd number**. Every `File*`, IoEngine's mirror table and the keep-alive slots all need no change, reads already in flight still hold the old file, and if they fail they re-read from the main drive as before.
   `IoEngine::readmit_source`: reopen each of this source's shard handles, then synchronously read 4 KiB from the middle of the file and compare it byte by byte against the same page on the main drive. Only if all of it agrees is the drop bit cleared and the consecutive-error count zeroed.
   If reads issued before the drop have not all come back, it does not pick it back up yet: the NVMe timeout is 30 s, longer than the 10 s of the first retry, and the failures of those old reads must not count against the new round's error allowance. The same once-per-second thread used by the temperature control (renamed `mirror_watch`, started whenever there is a mirror) does the scheduling: the first attempt 10 s after the drop, and the interval doubling on each failure and on each drop after a successful readmit, up to a ceiling of 10 min, so a drive that keeps erroring does not go in and out repeatedly. After picking back up, the sensor path is looked up again too (the drive changed name).
   A drop no longer zeroes the weight: `pick_source`, `pick_static` and striping all filter by the candidate mask first, so zeroing the weight was redundant anyway. `status`'s per-source line gains `readmitted Nx`. Not done on Windows (`File::reopen` returns Unimplemented: a Win32 handle is bound to one completion port for life).
   Unit tests:
   - `io.file_reopen_keeps_the_handle_and_reads_the_new_file`: after the same path becomes a new inode, the handle's value is unchanged and it reads the new content; a wrong length is refused and the handle stays as it was.
   - `io.dropped_mirror_is_readmitted_when_it_answers_again`: a probe page that differs from the main drive keeps it out; the first error after being picked back up is only a failover and does not get it kicked again; reads really do go to the mirror after it is picked back up; it is not picked back up while old reads are still in flight; and if the file is gone it stays out.
   - `io.stripe_is_the_default_and_leaves_backfill_whole`: now really routes once in the default state, with 16 chunks split across the two drives.
   Five mutations added: not zeroing the error count, not reopening, not comparing against the main drive, not waiting for old reads, and `reopen` not doing `dup3`. All are caught, and this io group is 11/11.
   **codex review** (read-only) raised four points, handled as follows:
   1. A file of the same length but different content would be picked back up → changed, the main-drive comparison was added.
   2. Old reads' failures would count against the new error allowance → changed, the in-flight check was added.
   3. A hung drive makes `stop()` wait for the kernel timeout → not changed, written into a comment: reads the dispatcher itself issued to that drive have to be waited on just the same.
   4. The tests did not test the essential part → the reopen test above and the one default routing case were added. Its claim that "replacing `reopen` with a no-op would still pass the tests" is wrong: the case where the file is deleted catches it, and the mutation confirmed it.
   Three other places it looked at with no findings: the routing after the weight zeroing was removed, the backoff algorithm and lock order, and the Windows stub.
   **On the GPU**: the 8-turn conversation of the readmit version (ledger #27, `d8f5644785` is the commit before codex's corrections; the corrections only touched `readmit_source`, which is not on the hot path) gives 9.269 tok/s, stall 41.8 ms, ms/miss 2.94. The differences against #26's 9.302 / 41.7 / 2.90 are within jitter, which says neither the resident watch thread nor the lock `add_mirror` added costs anything. The external drive peaked at 74 °C, took no break, and did not drop.
   **Picking back up after a real drive drop has not been encountered on the machine yet**, and has to wait for the next link drop to see `answers again` in the log.

0q. **Two drives (an external USB4 drive as a second read source, striped by chunk): conversation 9.291 tok/s (ledger #22, the same 8-turn script; single drive #24 7.682, +21%), stall 64.4 → 41.7 ms, prefill 66 → 42 s, end to end 6.26 → 7.90 tok/s (+26%). Temperature control added: the external drive rests when it reaches 80 °C and comes back once it drops to 72 °C.**
   **Numbers** (`bench/results/linux/multidisk/`, `DEEPMOE_MIRROR_AUTO=1 DEEPMOE_MIRROR_STRIPE=1`): ms/miss 4.66 → **2.92**, hit 0.9436 unchanged, probes 4.59 : 3.73 GB/s. The external drive idles at 63–67 °C,
   peaking at **74 °C** for the whole session (the alarm line is 90), without dropping and with zero errors on `src[1]`. The same mechanism as 0i (2026-09-28, +16.8%), measured this time on the IO path after 0p.
   **Temperature control** (`ThermalGate`, `storage/source_router.h`): `set_sources` follows the mirror root directory's `st_dev` through `/sys/dev/block/M:m` to the drive controller's `hwmon*/temp1_input`;
   a separate thread reads it once per second (an NVMe hwmon read is an admin command and cannot sit on the dispatcher), takes the source out of the candidate mask at `>= hot_c`, and only puts it back at `<= hot_c − 8` —
   the same AND as SourceHealth's drop, only reversible. The main drive never rests. `DEEPMOE_MIRROR_HOT_C` (default 80, 0 = off); `status`'s per-source P0 line gains "the current °C (max), how many times it has rested, RESTING" at the end.
   The unit test `io.thermal_gate_rests_a_hot_mirror_with_hysteresis`, with one mutation (the hysteresis removed). **End-to-end verification** (#25, the threshold temporarily 70 °C): it rose to 70 °C during the startup warm-up and was rested, P0 never went to the external drive again,
   and the whole session ran single-drive at 7.631 tok/s; it stopped at 64 °C and did not come down to 62 — **the threshold for coming back has to be above the idle temperature**, and the default 80 / 72 satisfies that (idle 63–67).
   **The two other gates already there**: the external drive losing the link mid-read ⇒ that request is re-read from the main drive (the caller only sees one slow read, D4/ST); 3 consecutive errors ⇒ kicked out within this process (SourceHealth).
   **Picking back up after a drop**: see 0r.
   **The default**: see 0r — striping has been on by default with a mirror since 2026-09-29.

0p. **IO: on Linux the IoEngine submits on the dispatcher's single thread (submit threads 8 → 1). The conversation (ledger #21, the same 8-turn script) 7.572 → 7.696 tok/s, ms/miss 4.79 → 4.62, stall 66.3 → 64.3 ms, hit 0.9439 unchanged; IO is now right against the drive's busy-time bandwidth.**
   **Why**: the submit thread pool was built for Windows' synchronous `ReadFile` into path A (704 µs/chunk) (Track Q2). Linux's io_uring submit is ~7 µs, and a read completion's task work runs on **the thread that submitted it**,
   so with a pool, every chunk completion first has to wake a sleeping submit thread before the dispatcher can reap it. `io_dst_bench` single expert (1 MiB × QD 8) p50: reading into RAM 5.17 / 5.05 → **4.75 / 4.73 ms**,
   reading into path A 5.17 / 5.14 → **4.63 / 4.64 ms** (ABAB, `bench/results/linux/iopath/submit_threads_ab.txt`) — level with the 8-thread `preadv` Python probe (4.74 back to back).
   `kDefaultSubmitThreads` takes its value per platform (8 on Windows, 1 elsewhere), and `DEEPMOE_IO_SUBMIT_THREADS` can still change it. 0n's "IO has reached the hardware's floor" was one step early: it took the Python probe (which reads from threads directly anyway) as the floor and did not measure the engine's own path.
   **The same binary with only this one switch flipped** (required by the codex review; #23 / #24, exe `b323add3` identical in both arms): 8 → 1 thread 7.562 → **7.682** tok/s, stall 66.5 → 64.4 ms, ms/miss 4.76 → **4.66** —
   consistent with the difference from #20 → #21, so it is this change's. The +1.6% in tok/s is within the ±3% jitter line, and what carries it is ms/miss (which moves only ~1% on a rerun) plus the single-expert ABAB.
   **What is below this (measured, none of it to be done)**: the model sits on **btrfs on dm-crypt (LUKS)**, and a read is decrypted by ~14 `kcryptd` threads and checksummed by `btrfs-endio`. The average read time in `/sys/block/{nvme0n1,dm-0}/stat`
   is only **18 µs / 126 KiB bio** higher for dm-0 than for nvme0n1 (1,436 vs 1,418 µs, ~1%; this is the difference of two devices' cumulative statistics and, under concurrency, is not the latency of an individual read), and AES-XTS single-core is 12.6 GB/s (`iopath/dmcrypt_latency.txt`) — things like `no_read_workqueue` are not worth touching the root partition for.
   The drive's busy-time bandwidth under this kind of bursty random 1 MiB reading is exactly **~4.0 GB/s** (io_ticks during the probe 3.97 GB/s; so perf_report's "4.8 GB/s floor" is a nominal value and is now labelled nominal), and the engine's 4.62 ms/miss = 4.08 GB/s. The SN740 has no DRAM, HMB has already been given the 64 MiB the drive asks for (the driver's ceiling is 128), and the NVMe `max_hw_sectors_kb` of 128 is the hardware's.
   All that is left is fewer misses and a second drive (stripe, 0i). **Capacity**: `hitrate_sim curve` (the trace of this conversation) 5,500 → 5,700 / 6,000 / 6,500 slots gives simulated hit 0.9315 → 0.9342 / 0.9377 / 0.9431,
   i.e. misses −4% / −9% / −17% (≈ −2.5 / −5.6 / −10 ms/token, 1–4% after halving), at a cost of +3.5 / +8.8 / +17.5 GiB. **The simulator is not calibrated**: at 5,500 slots it says 0.9315 while the measurement is 0.9411, and only 1,332 / 2,350 steps agree step by step, so these differences are only indicative; and at full load GTT is 107 / 112 GiB with only ~7 GiB left for the system — **whether to trade the memory headroom for it is left to the user** (`iopath/capacity_curve.json`). The policy is closed (§3's 47).
   **prefill** (the engine log now books it item by item, `bench/results/linux/prefill/prefill_breakdown.txt`): a new topic of 35 tokens takes 7.8 s = expert IO 6.53 s (1,619 experts at 4.66 GB/s, a continuous stream being faster than decode's bursts)
   + expert GPU 0.56 + attention 0.34 + the rest 0.34 (the log now lists the setup / KV seeding / teardown outside `Prefill::run` separately as `setup`, and the items add up to wall time). transit is already double-buffered (reading the next batch while computing this one); what does not overlap is the inter-layer dependency (the next layer's routing has to wait for this layer to be computed), worth about 1% end to end at most, not doing it.
   **On the compute side** (the `attn_bench` unit test, since the per-stage trace is unreadable after the wave split): wq_b 226, wo_a / wo_b ksplit 212 / 211, head 234 GB/s, with all the big kernels at 91–98% of bandwidth; the ~9 ms extra in a hot step is spread over ~1,100 dispatches, and fusing them one at a time is worth 0.1–0.5% each.

0o. **decode's index key changed to "each source layer uses its own" (2026-09-29, the user decided "each uses its own" and "but it has to be reused"), with the one cross-layer reuse untouched; the 4K / 17K oracles were re-exported under the same rule. Bit-exactness unchanged at ≤ 1,024 context.**
   **What changed**: the reference behaviour recorded in 0m — on a step where the ratio-2 group is not full, layers 2/8/14 score against **the previous step's layer 20** keys — is gone: every kv source (2/8/14/20) **scores against its own key cache on every step**, consistent with prefill.
   Reuse is as before: a non-source layer reads the nearest kv source's compressed KV and the nearest index source's top-k, and 24–36 score against layer 20's keys.
   **The implementation is a deletion**: `Engine::pub_index_k_` / `batch_snap_pub_` (the dynamic "last publisher" and its rollback snapshot) are deleted, and the key's source = `CedPlan::cmp_src`, fixed at startup;
   the batch path's `key_sel` and `kKCachePub` slot in `mgt1_idx.slang` are deleted, and `BatchStep`'s three fields own / pub / key_sel are merged into one `idx_key`.
   **The oracle**: `tools/oracle_longctx.py` adds a shim before `run_layer_decode` (a layer with `owns_k` points `shared.index_k` back at its own `k_cache`), with `model.py` untouched.
   On the Linux CPU, 4K takes 10 min and 17K 14.6 min (estimated 34), and the greedy tokens are the same as the old export (`kestrel-4471-amber"`); the old export is in `traces/longctx/refkeys/`. The new prefill export is not bit-identical to the Windows one (different CPU BLAS).
   **Gates**: `l3_ppl` off **0.622784 unchanged bit for bit** (n_cmp ≤ 512, so the rule does not take effect; `bench/results/linux/ownkeys/l3ppl.txt`).
   `decode_longctx.indexer_vs_reference`: on **every step** (including unfilled ones) the scores of layers 2/14/20 are bit-equal to the reference and the top-k is tie-aware equal. `engine_vs_reference`: at both lengths TF 8/8, salt 7/7, free generation 8/8.
   **One step below the 0.74 line**: 17K step 3 layer 20 attn_norm 0.697 (layer 14 gate 5/6 → layer 20 4/6). Under four variants that change only the fp32 summation order this step is **0.70 / 0.82 / 0.94 / 0.89**
   (default K-split / K-split off / dispatch B LB 32 RB 1 / both), which is the chaos of a near-tied routing, not a wrong rule. Under the original rule (the lowest − the range) the line is **0.74 → 0.55** (`bench/results/linux/ownkeys/variants/`).
   replay 128 (the conversation default), freely generating from our own prefilled state, is **8/8 at both 4K and 17K** (`p3_longctx_decode.md` §4.5); a full pass of all suites is 39 passing + `gpu_prefill` skipped (its longctx case needs the environment variable, i.e. the replay above; `ownkeys/ctest_suites.txt`, and the batch path is covered by multistream, spec_forward and kv_replay).
   The new oracle is overall further off than the old one (the worst attn_norm across variants 0.70–0.82, against 0.84–0.93 for the old): even on the rule-independent 4K step 0 loaded channel, layer 14's attn_norm went from 0.985 to 0.924 — that is how far the reference itself moves when its CPU numerics change.

0n. **The second full round of optimisation: on the IO side all three probes reached the drive's floor, and on the compute side mutually independent dispatches were split into waves plus mega_mhc's Sinkhorn was taken off the critical path. The conversation (ledger #20, the same 8-turn script) 7.264 → 7.572 tok/s, compute 68.3 → 62.5 ms; the hot step (#19) 67.36 → 66.01 ms (trace); the numerics are bit-identical.**
   **The compute side**. ① **Asynchronous engram** (`6384635`, #18): the 96 row reads of the two engram layers are issued at layer 0 and land when their layer comes, engram 4.2 → 2.1 ms/token.
   ② **Splitting the projections into waves** (`DecodeLayer::record_attention` / `record_ced(wave)`): previously every dispatch was followed by a barrier (a ~2.4 µs gap), but wq_a, wkv, the compressor's two projections and the indexer's head weights **read only u**;
   wq_b, the window KV write, the compression pooling and indexer.wq_b **read only the previous wave's output**; the index key, the indexer's q finish and the compression cache write **read the wave before that** (the cache write reads latent but does not change it). Hence three waves with one barrier each,
   with the small single-workgroup kernels running underneath wq_a / wq_b, and only then the indexer's scoring chain (wave 3, serial). By the trace's model: taking the max of each group would save 1.82 ms, and the bandwidth floor of bytes ÷ 225 GB/s is 1.17 ms; measured under the trace it is −0.67 ms — half again.
   ③ **mega_mhc's stage 2 (the RMSNorm producing u) never depended on stage 1 (mix)**, and mix and Sinkhorn are only used by the **next** sublayer. Sinkhorn is split out into an independent stage 3 (one workgroup, `MhcSinkhorn` / `MhcSinkhornB`),
   with the attention half's mix going into projection wave 0 and Sinkhorn into wave 1; on the FFN half, mix is in the same wave as gate.score and Sinkhorn in the same wave as gate.topk. The M = 1 path no longer needs `kMhcFlagSkipSinkhorn` (the tail collapse dispatches only stages 0 / 2; the flag is left for mgt1).
   The hot step without the trace 65.7 → 64.9 ms; under the trace 66.69 → 65.78. The two items together take dispatches 1021 → 1101 and barriers 2.77 → 2.57 ms.
   Gates: `l3_ppl` off **0.622784 / 56/64**, with the tokens and margins (to 4 decimal places) of all 64 steps **exactly the same** as before the change (`opt/l3ppl_waves_logs` against `l3ppl_engram_async_logs`, md5 matching; the finer logits were not compared bit for bit), `suite.decode` and `suite.gpu_attn` pass, the hot-step margin 7.0220 unchanged, CPU 25/25 + 30/30.
   **The basis (codex review)**: that 7.264 row is #16, whose conditions differ from #20's (during #16 another program wrote 34.6 GB to the disk and background GPU was 22.9%, against 41 MB / 12.1% for #20), so the conversation's +4.2% is only indicative; the controlled number is the hot step.
   **An instrument caveat**: on RADV the trace's end stamps (the COMPUTE_SHADER stage) serialise the stamps within one wave — the ms of the wave's first dispatch swallows the whole wave and the rest show 0.3 µs. **The span is right, but the per-stage ms within a wave is unreadable** (the 1.40 ms of "excess" for `mega_mhc.mix` in the `perf_report` table is this).
   **Tried and reverted**: changing gate.topk to count ranks in one scan (bit-identical) 8.1 → **23.9 µs** (384 dependent LDS reads), and 16.0 µs after unrolling it into two 16 B reads × 8, still losing to the 16-round wave reduction → reverted.
   gate's host-coherent progress counter (the host switched to a fence long ago and nobody reads it) is worth only 0.5 µs/layer, left alone. Merging `wo_b.kcombine` into `mega_mhc.post.ffn` (0.13 ms/token) would need a branch added to mhc, not doing it.
   **The IO side** (`bench/probes/idle_gap_probe.py`, O_DIRECT, 8 threads × 1 MiB, not touching the GPU; the results are in `bench/results/linux/opt/`). ① **The cost of waking an idle drive**: one expert (18.8 MB) takes 4.44–4.55 ms after an idle of ≤10 ms,
   5.83 after 30 ms and 6.50 after 100 ms; in a conversation the idle before a burst is 0–3 ms 32.9% of the time, 3–10 ms 44.1%, 10–30 ms 20.0%, ≥30 ms 3.0% (`chat_attncm/route.bin`, 10.1 bursts per step).
   The model works out to 1.20 ms per step; a spin + a 4 KiB poke every 5 ms could push it to 0.17 — a lever of ~1 ms/step = 0.7%, 0.4% after halving, below the jitter line, **not doing it**. ② **How to cut the reads**: after a 3 ms idle, 512 KiB × 16 gives 4.61, **1 MiB × 8 4.43**, 1 MiB × 16 4.39, 2 MiB × 8 4.66, 4 MiB × 4 5.44, 4 MiB × 8 5.91 ms — the default is already at the optimum.
   ③ **The real layout**: an expert is two extents (scales 1.1 MB + weights 17.7 MB, at two places in the shard). `expert_read_order_probe`: manifest order 4.47, weights first 4.50, tail blocks merged 4.41, scales alone 0.63, weights alone 4.16 ms —
   the burst bandwidth of 4.2–4.3 GB/s is just what this drive does, and both the order and the tail block are within noise. In the engine, stall ≈ the drive being busy (over 2,301 decode steps: 71.25 vs 70.61 ms/step), so of the ≤0.3 ms by which ms/miss (#20's 4.79) exceeds the probe, only ~0.1 is the wake-up. **The IO side has reached the hardware's floor.**
   **The next candidate, by size**: after the fence, have the host record the *next* layer's attention (61 µs a layer) before submitting *this* layer's MoE -- the hot step only exposes 5–9 µs of it (shared-early covers the rest), but a missing layer still pays ~55 µs after the IO lands;
   to pre-record while waiting on the gate fence, attention's address tables have to be double-buffered (`bind` changes the slots the layer currently running reads). Estimated at ~0.9 ms/token in a conversation, halved 0.3%. shared-early is at 79% of its ceiling, but it is covered by the host round trip, so speeding it up alone buys nothing.

0m. **A review of V4.1's inference form (how each layer computes, and how KV is fetched), replay 128 verified token by token on a long context for the first time, and the Linux long-prompt device loss fixed.**
   **Layer by layer against the reference** (`inference/model.py`; all of it is implemented, and the tests compare against an **unmodified** `model.py` -- the CPU shell in `tools/dsref.py`):
   the layer types are `compress_ratios` = [0,0, 2×18, 1×20]; ratio 0 is pure window, theta 1e4, no YaRN, and ratio > 0 is theta 1.6e5 + YaRN (16 / 65536 / 32 / 1) (`runtime/rope.h`);
   mHC's attention uses the **previous layer's FFN** `pre_mix` (one-hot at layer 0), the FFN uses this layer's attention one, and the tail uses the last layer's; engram is added onto the hc stream **before** the blocks of layers 1 and 14;
   the window KV is a 128-entry ring per layer (E4M3 + UE8M0/32, RoPE tail included; oldest first in decode, unfilled as −1); the compressed KV and the index keys live on **one plane each at 2/8/14/20 only**
   (block16 + E4M3 / block32 + E8M0, stored as dequantised bf16, bit-equivalent), and the RoPE for the latent and the key takes the **group's first** position, `pos + 1 − ratio`;
